[Planning] Overall plan
============================================
Below is a practical reproduction plan for **“On Embeddings for Numerical Features in Tabular Deep Learning”** focused on faithfully rebuilding the paper’s methodology and experimental protocol from scratch.

---

# 1) Reproduction goal

The paper studies how **numerical-feature embeddings** affect tabular deep learning models, especially:

- **MLP**
- **ResNet**
- **Transformer / FT-Transformer**
- Compared against **CatBoost** and **XGBoost**

The core claim is that better numerical embeddings can substantially improve tabular DL performance, and in some cases close the gap to GBDT.

Your reproduction should therefore implement:

1. The **embedding modules** described in Section 3:
   - Simple linear embedding
   - **Piecewise Linear Encoding (PLE)**
   - **Periodic activations**
   - Baselines such as AutoDis / DICE if desired for completeness
2. The **same backbone families**:
   - MLP
   - ResNet
   - Transformer-L (FT-Transformer)
3. The **same evaluation protocol**:
   - Dataset-specific preprocessing
   - Hyperparameter tuning on validation
   - 15 random seeds for each tuned configuration
   - Mean test performance reported
   - Ensemble evaluation for some comparisons
4. The same **dataset set**:
   - 11 public tabular datasets

---

# 2) Methodology to reproduce

## 2.1 General framework for numerical embeddings

The paper defines a per-feature embedding function:

- For each numerical feature \(x_i\), compute
  \[
  z_i = f_i(x_i) \in \mathbb{R}^{d_i}
  \]
- Important constraints:
  - Each feature is embedded **independently**
  - The same functional form is used across all features
  - Parameters are **not shared** between features
- Output usage:
  - For **MLP-like** backbones: concatenate all feature embeddings into one flat vector
  - For **Transformer-like** backbones: feed embeddings as token-like feature vectors directly

This is crucial: do **not** mix numerical features inside the embedding function itself, except where the backbone later mixes them.

---

## 2.2 Piecewise Linear Encoding (PLE)

This is one of the paper’s main contributions.

### Core idea
For each numerical feature, split its range into bins and encode a scalar value as a piecewise-linear vector over bins.

### Encoding behavior
For a feature value \(x\) and bins \(B_1,\dots,B_T\), the encoding is a vector of length \(T\). Each component is:

- 0 before the active interval
- 1 after the active interval
- linearly interpolated inside the active bin

This makes the representation:
- continuous
- ordered
- more expressive than scalar input
- still relatively interpretable

### Important implementation note
For **Transformer-based models**, PLE alone is not enough because Transformers are permutation-invariant over token order. The paper solves this by placing a **linear layer after PLE** so each feature’s bins are mapped to a trainable embedding space.

Formally:
- PLE(x) → Linear → final embedding

This is equivalent to having trainable vectors for bins and aggregating them with PLE weights.

---

## 2.3 Bin construction for PLE

The paper uses two binning strategies:

### A. Quantile-based bins
- Bins are formed using empirical quantiles of each feature on the **training split**
- Use the training set only to compute quantiles
- Remove empty bins if any

This is the unsupervised version: **PLE-q**

### B. Target-aware bins
- Bins are built using labels
- The method is analogous to **C4.5 discretization**
- Practically, for each feature, build a greedy 1D decision tree on the training data using the target
- Leaves define bins
- Also set:
  - \(b_0 = \min(x)\)
  - \(b_T = \max(x)\)

This is the supervised version: **PLE-t**

### Hyperparameters to tune
For target-aware PLE:
- maximum number of leaves
- minimum number of items per leaf
- minimum information gain required for a split

For quantile PLE:
- number of quantiles

---

## 2.4 Periodic activation embeddings

The paper also adapts periodic features for tabular data.

### Key design choice
Unlike some coordinate-based periodic approaches, the paper insists that each feature be embedded **separately before mixing**.

### Trainable form
- Uses trainable pre-activation coefficients
- Initialize coefficients from \( \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma) \)
- Tune:
  - \(\sigma\)
  - \(k\) (number of periodic components / embedding size factor, as described in the paper)

The exact mathematical equation is not fully visible in the provided parse, but the implementation should follow the repository/paper formula as closely as possible:
- periodic transform per feature
- trainable coefficient initialization
- feature-wise independent embedding

This module is named **Periodic** in the experiments.

---

## 2.5 Simple differentiable layers on top of embeddings

The paper tests whether standard layers improve embeddings:

- Linear
- ReLU
- LeakyReLU
- stacked combinations like:
  - Linear
  - Linear + ReLU
  - Linear + ReLU + Linear
  - etc.

Important naming convention from the paper:
- **L** = linear layer
- **LR** = linear layer + ReLU
- **LRLR** = linear + ReLU + linear + ReLU
- **P** = periodic base module
- **PL** = periodic + linear
- **PLR** = periodic + linear + ReLU
- **PLE q / PLE t** similarly combined with linear/relu

For Transformer models, the paper often uses a linear layer after PLE to restore a token embedding dimension.

---

# 3) Datasets and data handling

## 3.1 Datasets used

The paper uses 11 datasets:

1. Gesture Phase Prediction (GE)
2. Churn Modelling (CH)
3. California Housing (CA)
4. House 16H (HO)
5. Adult (AD)
6. Otto Group Product Classification (OT)
7. Higgs Small (HI)
8. Facebook Comments Volume (FB)
9. Santander Customer Transaction Prediction (SA)
10. Covertype (CO)
11. Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K fold 1 (MI)

From the paper’s table, these include a mix of:
- binary classification
- multiclass classification
- regression
- ranking treated as regression for Microsoft

### Dataset-specific properties
The paper includes numbers of:
- train / validation / test samples
- numerical features
- categorical features
- task type
- batch size

You should reconstruct these exactly from Appendix C / Table 8 if possible.

---

## 3.2 Preprocessing protocol

The paper is explicit:

### For deep models
- Use the **same preprocessing for all deep models** on a given dataset
- For all datasets **except Otto Group Product Classification**, apply **quantile transformation** from scikit-learn
- For Otto, **do not apply feature preprocessing**
- Regression targets are standardized for all algorithms

### For categorical features
- For CatBoost: use built-in categorical feature support
- For all other algorithms: one-hot encode categorical variables

This is important for fidelity.

### What to implement
Build a preprocessing pipeline that can switch by dataset:
- Quantile transform on numerical features
- No preprocessing for Otto
- One-hot encode categorical features for non-CatBoost methods
- Target standardization for regression

---

## 3.3 Splits

The dataset is split into:

- train
- validation
- test

The paper says it follows Gorishniy et al. for split construction and that the split script is provided in supplementary material.

You should:
- Recreate the same split logic if available from the referenced paper/code
- Otherwise, use a deterministic train/val/test split protocol and document the limitation

Critical point:
- validation set is used for **hyperparameter tuning and early stopping**
- test set is used **only once after tuning**

---

# 4) Backbones to implement

The paper uses three main backbones.

## 4.1 MLP
Standard feed-forward network on concatenated feature embeddings.

Tune:
- number of layers
- layer size
- dropout
- learning rate
- weight decay

From the appendix:
- # layers: UniformInt[1, 16]
- layer size: UniformInt[1, 1024]
- dropout: {0, Uniform[0, 0.5]}
- learning rate: LogUniform[5e-5, 0.005]
- weight decay: {0, LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-3]}
- # iterations: 100

## 4.2 ResNet
Tabular ResNet as in Gorishniy et al.

Tune:
- # layers: UniformInt[1, 8]
- layer size: UniformInt[32, 512]
- hidden factor: Uniform[1, 4]
- hidden dropout: Uniform[0, 0.5]
- residual dropout: {0, Uniform[0, 0.5]}
- learning rate: LogUniform[5e-5, 0.005]
- weight decay: {0, LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-3]}
- # iterations: 100

## 4.3 Transformer-L
This is FT-Transformer from Gorishniy et al.

Tune:
- # layers
- embedding size
- residual dropout
- attention dropout
- FFN dropout
- FFN factor
- learning rate
- weight decay

The paper provides dataset-dependent search spaces:
- For datasets {SA, CO, MI}:
  - # layers: UniformInt[2, 4]
  - embedding size: UniformInt[192, 512]
  - residual dropout: Const(0.0)
  - attention dropout: Uniform[0, 0.5]
  - FFN dropout: Uniform[0, 0.5]
  - FFN factor: Uniform[2/3, 8/3]
  - learning rate: LogUniform[1e-5, 3e-4]
  - weight decay: Const(1e-5)
- For the rest:
  - # layers: UniformInt[1, 4]
  - embedding size: UniformInt[96, 512]
  - residual dropout: {0, Uniform[0, 0.2]}
  - attention dropout: Uniform[0, 0.5]
  - FFN dropout: Uniform[0, 0.5]
  - FFN factor: Uniform[2/3, 8/3]
  - learning rate: LogUniform[1e-5, 1e-3]
  - weight decay: LogUniform[1e-6, 1e-4]

# 5) Model variants to reproduce

The paper uses a naming scheme of:

**Backbone-Embedding**

You should implement these families:

## 5.1 Simple embedding baselines
- MLP
- MLP-L
- MLP-LR
- MLP-LRLR
- MLP-AutoDis
- MLP-DICE

Likewise for ResNet and Transformer-L where applicable.

## 5.2 PLE-based
- MLP-Q
- MLP-Q-LR
- MLP-Q-LRLR
- MLP-T
- MLP-T-LR
- MLP-T-LRLR

Similarly:
- ResNet-Q, ResNet-Q-LR, ResNet-Q-LRLR
- ResNet-T, ResNet-T-LR, ResNet-T-LRLR
- Transformer-Q-L, Transformer-Q-LR
- Transformer-T-L, Transformer-T-LR

## 5.3 Periodic-based
- MLP-P
- MLP-PL
- MLP-PLR
- ResNet-P
- ResNet-PLR
- Transformer-PLR

The paper often reports that:
- PLE + linear/relu often improves performance
- Periodic + differentiable layers tends to outperform raw periodic alone

# 6) Training and optimization protocol

## 6.1 Optimizer
Use:
- **AdamW**

## 6.2 Loss
- Classification: cross-entropy
- Regression: mean squared error

## 6.3 Learning rate schedules
- **No learning rate schedules**

## 6.4 Early stopping
Train until:
- `patience + 1` consecutive epochs without validation improvement
- `patience = 16`

## 6.5 Epoch budget
The appendix lists `# iterations = 100` for many hyperparameter searches, likely meaning a maximum of 100 training epochs/iterations in tuning runs. The exact interpretation should match the original codebase as closely as possible.

## 6.6 Seeds and repetition
For each tuned configuration:
- run **15 experiments with different random seeds**
- report average test performance

This is required.

---

# 7) Hyperparameter tuning strategy

## 7.1 Tuning method
The paper uses:
- **Optuna**
- TPE sampler / Bayesian optimization

## 7.2 Validation selection
- Tune on validation score only
- Never use test set during tuning

## 7.3 Search spaces to implement

### Linear embedding output size
- UniformInt[1, 128]

### PLE quantile bins
- UniformInt[2, 256]

### PLE target-aware bins
- max leaves: UniformInt[2, 256]
- min samples per leaf: UniformInt[1, 128]
- min information gain: LogUniform[1e-9, 0.01]

### Periodic module
- \(k\): UniformInt[1, 128]
- \(\sigma\): tuned, but the exact search space is not explicitly visible in the parse
  - You should inspect supplementary material or repository if available
  - This is a point of ambiguity to note in your reproduction log

---

# 8) Evaluation protocol

## 8.1 Metrics
Use the metric appropriate for each dataset:
- **Accuracy** for classification
- **RMSE** for regression

For ranking dataset Microsoft:
- treat as regression, per the paper

## 8.2 Reporting
For each tuned model:
- run 15 seeds
- report mean test performance
- also compute standard deviations

## 8.3 Ensemble evaluation
For each model-dataset pair:
- split the 15 trained models into **3 disjoint groups of 5**
- average predictions within each group
- report ensemble performance

This is used in the broader DL vs GBDT comparison.

## 8.4 Rank aggregation
The paper reports average rank across datasets. If you want to match the tables:
- rank models within each dataset
- better metric value ranks higher for accuracy
- lower ranks higher for RMSE
- average ranks across datasets

---

# 10) Implementation roadmap

## Step 1: Reconstruct data loaders
Build dataset-specific loaders that can:
- fetch or read raw data
- identify categorical vs numerical columns
- apply split logic
- apply preprocessing
- return train/val/test tensors

## Step 2: Implement preprocessing pipeline
Per dataset:
- quantile transform numerical features, except Otto
- one-hot encode categorical features for non-CatBoost algorithms
- standardize regression targets

## Step 3: Implement embedding modules
Create a clean abstraction:
- `NumericalEmbeddingBase`
- `LinearEmbedding`
- `PiecewiseLinearEncoding`
- `PeriodicEmbedding`
- `AutoDisEmbedding`
- `DICEEmbedding` if included

Each should:
- operate feature-wise
- support per-feature independent parameters
- provide output shape compatible with MLP / Transformer backbones

## Step 4: Implement backbones
- MLP
- ResNet
- FT-Transformer / Transformer-L

Ensure model wrappers accept:
- raw numerical inputs
- embedded numerical inputs
- categorical inputs if needed

## Step 5: Build model variants
Compose backbone + embedding according to the naming scheme.

## Step 6: Implement tuning engine
Use Optuna TPE:
- define search spaces exactly as paper
- tune on validation
- save best hyperparameters per model-dataset pair

## Step 7: Implement training loop
Features:
- AdamW
- no LR schedule
- early stopping with patience 16
- consistent seed control
- checkpoint best validation model

## Step 8: Evaluation and ensembling
- Run 15 seeds for each best config
- Evaluate mean/std on test
- Build 3 ensembles of 5 models for ensemble comparisons

## Step 9: Produce tables
- Match the paper’s tables:
  - model comparison
  - dataset results
  - parameter counts
  - preprocessing sensitivity
  - alternative encodings

   - The paper says \(\sigma\) is tuned, but the visible appendix excerpt does not show the distribution

4. **Meaning of `# iterations = 100`**
   - Likely max training epochs in hyperparameter tuning; confirm with implementation details from the original benchmark protocol

5. **Exact backbone implementation details**
   - Especially FT-Transformer / ResNet differences relative to the Gorishniy et al. codebase

# 12) Practical reproduction priority order

If you want the fastest faithful reproduction, do it in this order:

1. **Data preprocessing + splits**
2. **Baseline MLP / ResNet / Transformer-L**
3. **Linear embedding baseline**
4. **PLE-q and PLE-t**
5. **Periodic embedding**
6. **Optuna tuning**
7. **15-seed evaluation**
8. **Ensembles and rank tables**
9. **Optional: AutoDis, DICE, thermometer, one-blob**

This order gives you a working pipeline early, then lets you add modules incrementally.

metrics\n    end\n\n    M-->>U: save tables, metrics, checkpoints",
  "Anything UNCLEAR": "The exact periodic-embedding equation and the precise search space for sigma are not fully visible in the parsed paper text, so those should be confirmed from the supplementary material or source repository if available. Finally, the paper’s mention of 'iterations = 100' in tuning tables should be verified to determine whether it means training epochs, optimization steps, or another stopping budget."

============================================
## Logic Analysis: `utils/reproducibility.py`

### Purpose
This module must provide the foundational reproducibility controls used by every other part of the experiment pipeline. It is the first module to implement because:

- all dataset splitting must be deterministic,
- all hyperparameter tuning trials must be reproducible,
- all model initialization and training runs must be seeded consistently,
- all evaluation results must be comparable across the 15 runs required by the paper,
- device selection must be centralized and explicit.

Per the design, this module exposes exactly these public functions:

- `set_seed(seed)`
- `set_deterministic()`
- `get_device(device)`

No other public API should be assumed.

---

### Paper-driven requirements
From the paper and `config.yaml`, the reproducibility policy is:

- A single global experiment seed is defined in `config.experiment.seed = 42`.
- Each tuned configuration must be evaluated over `config.experiment.num_seeds = 15` random seeds.
- Training must use early stopping with `patience = 16` and no LR schedule.
- All training/tuning/evaluation must be deterministic as far as PyTorch allows.
- Reproducibility must cover:
  - Python `random`
  - NumPy
  - PyTorch CPU
  - PyTorch CUDA, if available

The module should not invent any additional randomness policy or seed schedule; it only establishes the deterministic base and will be called by higher-level code that varies seeds across the 15 runs.

---

### Responsibilities of each function

#### 1) `set_seed(seed)`
This function must seed all relevant RNGs using the provided integer seed.

Required behavior:
- seed Python’s `random`
- seed NumPy’s RNG
- seed PyTorch CPU RNG
- seed PyTorch CUDA RNGs if CUDA is available
- optionally set environment variable `PYTHONHASHSEED` for hash-based determinism

Important implementation constraints:
- Use only the seed passed in by the caller.
- Do not derive new seeds internally.
- Do not hardcode seed values.
- Do not assume a seed sequence beyond what higher-level orchestration provides.

Why this matters for the paper:
- The paper reports averages over 15 random seeds.
- Each of those runs must be independently reproducible once its seed is known.
- Optuna trials, model initializations, and minibatch shuffling must all respond to this seed.

---

#### 2) `set_deterministic()`
This function should enforce deterministic behavior in PyTorch as much as possible.

Required behavior:
- enable deterministic algorithms in PyTorch where supported
- disable CuDNN benchmarking
- enable CuDNN deterministic mode
- if needed, set environment variables that promote deterministic CUDA behavior

This function should be called once at startup, before model creation or data loading, so that:
- convolution and reduction kernels are deterministic where possible,
- GPU-related nondeterminism is minimized,
- repeated runs are stable.

Important constraints:
- Do not add training logic here.
- Do not couple this function to any dataset or model behavior.
- It is a global execution policy, not a trainer utility.

Potential practical note:
- Some deterministic settings may raise warnings or errors for unsupported operations. The module should prefer strict determinism, consistent with reproduction goals.

---

#### 3) `get_device(device)`
This function should convert a configuration string into a concrete PyTorch device.

Expected behavior:
- if `device` requests CUDA and CUDA is available, return a CUDA device
- otherwise return CPU
- if the caller passes a specific CUDA index, honor it when possible
- if the requested device is unavailable, fall back safely to CPU

This function must not:
- silently change model logic,
- allocate tensors,
- make policy decisions beyond device resolution.

Why this matters:
- The same experiment code should run on CPU or GPU depending on config and availability.
- Baseline GBDT and deep models may be executed on different hardware, but the device resolution for PyTorch models must be centralized.

---

### Interaction with `config.yaml`
This module should be driven indirectly by configuration, not by hardcoded assumptions.

Relevant config values:
- `experiment.seed`: used by `set_seed`
- `notes.implementation_constraints`: require deterministic reproduction
- training, tuning, and evaluation modules will call these utilities, but this module should not read the config file itself unless the surrounding architecture explicitly passes values in

Do **not** hardcode:
- `42`
- number of seeds
- patience
- batch sizes
- any dataset-specific choice

These belong to other modules and to the config-driven orchestration.

---

### Determinism considerations
To support the paper’s 15-seed protocol, the module should account for the following:

1. **Python hash determinism**
   - Setting `PYTHONHASHSEED` helps keep dictionary/set iteration more stable across runs.

2. **NumPy randomness**
   - Needed for preprocessing, split generation, and some tuning/sample procedures.

3. **PyTorch CPU and CUDA**
   - Model initialization
   - DataLoader shuffling
   - dropout masks
   - optimizer behavior

4. **GPU nondeterminism**
   - Some PyTorch operations may still be nondeterministic depending on backend support.
   - The module should do its best via deterministic flags, but higher-level code must avoid nondeterministic operations when possible.

---

### Expected call flow
This module is used at the very start of experiment execution:

1. `main.py` loads config.
2. `main.py` calls `set_seed(config.experiment.seed)`.
3. `main.py` calls `set_deterministic()`.
4. `main.py` resolves the execution device via `get_device(config.device)` or the relevant config field.
5. All subsequent modules inherit this deterministic context.

For repeated seed runs:
- higher-level code should call `set_seed(run_seed)` before each independent training/evaluation run.
- `set_deterministic()` can be called once globally at startup.

---

### Edge cases and expected handling

#### Invalid seed input
- The function should expect an integer-like seed.
- If the caller passes a non-integer, the safest behavior is to let the error surface clearly rather than silently coercing unexpected values.

#### CUDA unavailable
- `get_device("cuda")` should gracefully fall back to CPU if CUDA is not available.
- `set_seed` should still seed CPU RNGs normally.

#### Deterministic algorithm support
- Some PyTorch versions may warn if deterministic algorithms are not fully supported.
- The implementation should prioritize reproducibility over permissive execution.

---

### Testing implications
This module is small but critical, so it should be validated with simple checks:

- repeated calls to `set_seed(42)` produce the same:
  - NumPy random numbers
  - Python random numbers
  - PyTorch random tensors
- `get_device("cpu")` returns CPU
- `get_device("cuda")` returns CUDA only when available, otherwise CPU
- `set_deterministic()` does not crash on the target environment

---

### Implementation scope summary
`utils/reproducibility.py` should be a minimal, reliable foundation with no experiment-specific logic beyond deterministic execution and device resolution. It must support all later modules, especially:

- dataset split reproducibility
- Optuna tuning reproducibility
- repeated seed training
- fair evaluation and ensemble averaging

[ANALYSIS] utils/io.py
============================================
# Logic Analysis: `utils/io.py`

This module is the configuration and persistence hub for the entire reproduction pipeline. It must support:

- loading the experiment configuration from `config.yaml`
- serializing results, tuned hyperparameters, predictions, and checkpoints metadata
- deserializing previously saved artifacts
- providing a typed, reproducible config object that downstream modules can rely on

It is a foundational utility because every experiment path in the paper depends on consistent configuration handling.

---

## 1) Role of this module in the reproduction system

`utils/io.py` should be responsible for two separate concerns:

### A. Configuration loading
It must read the provided `config.yaml` and convert it into a structured experiment configuration object or dictionary.

This config object must preserve the hierarchy and values exactly as specified in `config.yaml`, because the reproduction must not invent any values. The module should serve as the single source of truth for:

- experiment seed control
- number of seeds and ensemble settings
- preprocessing policy
- training settings
- tuning search spaces
- model hyperparameter ranges
- baseline settings
- evaluation settings
- implementation constraints

### B. Artifact persistence
It must save and load experiment artifacts in a format that is easy to reuse across the pipeline, including:

- tuned hyperparameters
- validation/test metrics
- predictions
- ensemble outputs
- training logs
- possibly model metadata or checkpoint references

The module must not implement training or evaluation logic itself; it only provides reliable I/O utilities.

---

## 2) Configuration schema to support

The paper and config define a nested schema that must be preserved faithfully. The loader should not flatten or reinterpret it.

The expected top-level fields are:

- `experiment`
- `data`
- `training`
- `hyperparameter_tuning`
- `models`
- `evaluation`
- `baselines`
- `notes`

### 2.1 Required `experiment` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `seed`
- `num_seeds`
- `num_ensembles`
- `ensemble_group_size`

These are used by:
- `utils/reproducibility.py`
- `training/trainer.py`
- `evaluation/ensemble.py`
- `main.py`

### 2.2 Required `data` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `data.preprocessing.numerical.default`
- `data.preprocessing.numerical.otto_group_product_classification`
- `data.preprocessing.categorical.default`
- `data.target.regression_standardization`

These must drive preprocessing decisions exactly as the paper describes:
- quantile transform by default
- no numerical preprocessing for Otto
- one-hot encoding for categorical features
- target standardization for regression

### 2.3 Required `training` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `optimizer`
- `loss.classification`
- `loss.regression`
- `learning_rate.mlp`
- `learning_rate.resnet`
- `learning_rate.transformer.sa_co_mi`
- `learning_rate.transformer.other`
- `batch_size.*` for all datasets
- `early_stopping.patience`
- `early_stopping.min_delta`
- `lr_schedule`
- `max_epochs`

These values are referenced by:
- `training/trainer.py`
- `training/tuning.py`
- `models/backbones.py`
- `main.py`

### 2.4 Required `hyperparameter_tuning` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `method`
- `trials`
- `search_spaces.linear_embedding_output_dim`
- `search_spaces.ple_quantiles`
- `search_spaces.ple_tree.max_leaves`
- `search_spaces.ple_tree.min_items_per_leaf`
- `search_spaces.ple_tree.min_information_gain`
- `search_spaces.periodic_k`

These must be accessible unchanged because tuning logic must use them directly.

### 2.5 Required `models` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `models.mlp.layers`
- `models.mlp.layer_size`
- `models.mlp.dropout`
- `models.mlp.weight_decay`
- `models.resnet.layers`
- `models.resnet.layer_size`
- `models.resnet.hidden_factor`
- `models.resnet.hidden_dropout`
- `models.resnet.residual_dropout`
- `models.resnet.weight_decay`
- `models.transformer.layers.sa_co_mi`
- `models.transformer.layers.other`
- `models.transformer.embedding_size.sa_co_mi`
- `models.transformer.embedding_size.other`
- `models.transformer.residual_dropout.sa_co_mi`
- `models.transformer.residual_dropout.other`
- `models.transformer.attention_dropout`
- `models.transformer.ffn_dropout`
- `models.transformer.ffn_factor`
- `models.transformer.weight_decay.sa_co_mi`
- `models.transformer.weight_decay.other`

These should be used by `training/tuning.py` and `models/backbones.py`.

### 2.6 Required `evaluation` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `metrics.classification`
- `metrics.regression`
- `validation_selection`
- `test_runs_per_tuned_config`

These determine:
- validation selection criteria
- metric computation
- repeated evaluation runs

### 2.7 Required `baselines` fields
From `config.yaml`:

- `baselines.catboost.fixed.early_stopping_rounds`
- `baselines.catboost.fixed.od_pval`
- `baselines.catboost.fixed.iterations`
- `baselines.catboost.tuning.max_depth`
- `baselines.catboost.tuning.learning_rate`
- `baselines.catboost.tuning.bagging_temperature`
- `baselines.catboost.tuning.l2_leaf_reg`
- `baselines.catboost.tuning.leaf_estimation_iterations`
- `baselines.xgboost.fixed.booster`
- `baselines.xgboost.fixed.early_stopping_rounds`
- `baselines.xgboost.fixed.n_estimators`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.max_depth`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.min_child_weight`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.subsample`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.learning_rate`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.colsample_bytree`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.gamma`
- `baselines.xgboost.tuning.lambda`

These are consumed by `baselines/gbdt.py`.

---

## 3) Interface responsibilities

The task specifies these public functions:

- `load_config(path)`
- `save_json(obj, path)`
- `save_pickle(obj, path)`
- `load_pickle(path)`

This module must provide these functions exactly.

### 3.1 `load_config(path)`
This function should:

1. Accept a file path to YAML or JSON
2. Parse the file content
3. Return a structured config object

The returned object should be suitable for direct use by the rest of the pipeline. It can be either:
- a nested dictionary, or
- a typed dataclass / pydantic object

Given the design and shared knowledge, a typed object is preferable for consistency, but the implementation must remain lightweight.

#### Logic requirements
- preserve nested structure
- keep numeric values exactly as given in `config.yaml`
- do not infer missing values
- do not apply defaults unless explicitly encoded in the config
- allow access to nested fields in a stable way

#### Recommended behavior
- detect file extension or attempt YAML parsing first, then JSON fallback
- support `.yaml`, `.yml`, and `.json`
- return an `ExperimentConfig`-like object or a nested dict matching the config schema

### 3.2 `save_json(obj, path)`
This function should serialize Python objects to JSON for:
- hyperparameter summaries
- validation/test metrics
- ensemble scores
- experiment metadata

#### Logic requirements
- create parent directories if needed
- convert non-JSON-native objects using a safe serializer
- ensure deterministic formatting if possible
- do not silently lose important numeric precision

### 3.3 `save_pickle(obj, path)`
This function should save arbitrary Python objects, especially:
- trained model metadata
- split objects
- preprocessing objects
- prediction arrays
- Optuna study objects if needed

#### Logic requirements
- create parent directories if needed
- use standard pickle protocol
- be compatible with NumPy arrays, pandas objects, and model-related objects

### 3.4 `load_pickle(path)`
This function should load previously saved pickle artifacts.

#### Logic requirements
- exact inverse of `save_pickle`
- validate path existence before loading
- return the original object

---

## 4) Data structures and config handling strategy

Although the design allows a dict-like config, the module should aim for a lightweight typed structure to improve reproducibility and reduce accidental key mismatches.

### 4.1 Suggested config representation
Use a dataclass-based object or nested dataclass tree, if implemented cleanly.

At minimum, the loaded config should provide access to:
- `config.experiment.seed`
- `config.training.max_epochs`
- `config.models.transformer.layers.other`
- etc.

If a fully typed nested dataclass tree is too cumbersome, a nested mapping with helper access is acceptable, but it must remain stable and explicit.

### 4.2 Preservation of raw values
The loader should not coerce values beyond what parsing naturally yields.

Examples:
- `5.0e-05` should load as float
- `[1, 16]` should remain a two-element list
- `0.0` should remain float
- `"gbtree"` should remain string

### 4.3 No invented defaults
The config file already specifies:
- seed = 42
- num_seeds = 15
- patience = 16
- max_epochs = 100
- etc.

The module must not invent replacements if a key is missing. Instead:
- either raise a clear error
- or return the loaded data as-is and let downstream modules validate required fields

For reproducibility, the safer choice is to validate required keys and fail early if anything is missing.

---

## 5) Validation logic that should exist in `load_config`

The configuration file is central to the experiment. The loader should validate the presence of all required top-level sections and key nested paths.

### 5.1 Required top-level sections
Validate that the config contains:
- `experiment`
- `data`
- `training`
- `hyperparameter_tuning`
- `models`
- `evaluation`
- `baselines`

`notes` can be treated as optional metadata, though it is present.

### 5.2 Required consistency checks
The loader should check that:

- `experiment.num_seeds` matches the paper’s 15-seed evaluation protocol
- `experiment.num_ensembles` and `experiment.ensemble_group_size` are consistent
- `training.lr_schedule` is `none`
- `training.early_stopping.patience` is present and matches the config
- `data.preprocessing.numerical.otto_group_product_classification` is `none`
- `evaluation.test_runs_per_tuned_config` equals the number of repeated seed evaluations
- `training.loss.classification` and `training.loss.regression` are both specified
- all dataset batch sizes are present
- transformer-specific dataset group keys (`sa_co_mi`, `other`) exist

These checks ensure the rest of the system can rely on a complete config.

---

## 6) Serialization requirements for reproducibility

This module is not just for configs; it also supports the experiment lifecycle.

### 6.1 JSON outputs to support
Use JSON for:
- tuned hyperparameters per model-dataset pair
- validation results
- test results
- rank tables
- ensemble metrics
- experiment manifests

These are human-readable and easy to compare between runs.

### 6.2 Pickle outputs to support
Use pickle for:
- cached datasets or processed splits
- preprocessing transformers
- Optuna studies
- prediction arrays if needed
- checkpoint metadata if storing non-torch objects

For actual model weights, PyTorch checkpoint saving may happen elsewhere, but `save_pickle` can still store associated metadata.

---

## 7) Interaction with the rest of the pipeline

This module must integrate cleanly with all other modules in the design.

### 7.1 `main.py`
`main.py` should call:
- `load_config(config_path)`
- `save_json(...)` for outputs
- `save_pickle(...)` for caches or studies if needed

### 7.2 `utils/reproducibility.py`
Configuration loading happens before:
- `set_seed(config.experiment.seed)`
- `set_deterministic()`

So `load_config()` must return the seed value in a directly accessible location.

### 7.3 `training/tuning.py`
The tuner needs:
- tuning method
- trial count
- search spaces
- model ranges
- dataset-dependent transformer ranges

The config loader must preserve all of these nested structures exactly.

### 7.4 `baselines/gbdt.py`
GBDT baseline code depends on:
- fixed CatBoost/XGBoost settings
- tuning parameter ranges
- dataset-specific GPU/CPU behavior from the paper’s logic

The module should simply expose these settings unchanged.

---

## 8) Path handling requirements

All I/O functions should work robustly across environments.

### 8.1 `pathlib.Path` support
Accept both strings and `Path` objects.

### 8.2 Parent directory creation
For save functions:
- create parent directories automatically if they do not exist

### 8.3 Existence checks
For load functions:
- validate that the file exists
- raise a clear error if not found

### 8.4 Extension handling
`load_config()` should support:
- `.yaml`
- `.yml`
- `.json`

For output functions:
- `save_json` should ensure `.json` output paths are accepted
- `save_pickle` can use any suffix, but `.pkl` or `.pickle` is recommended

---

## 9) Determinism and reproducibility considerations

Although this module does not set seeds itself, it affects reproducibility through deterministic config handling and artifact storage.

### 9.1 Stable config parsing
Repeated loads of the same config file should yield equivalent structures.

### 9.2 Stable serialization
When saving JSON:
- use stable key ordering if possible
- use indentation to make outputs auditable
- avoid nondeterministic formatting

### 9.3 Artifact traceability
Saved outputs should ideally include:
- dataset name
- model name
- seed
- timestamp or run ID if handled externally

If `utils/io.py` does not generate run IDs itself, it should at least support saving to structured paths chosen by `main.py`.

---

## 10) Error handling strategy

The module should fail fast and clearly.

### 10.1 Config parsing errors
If YAML/JSON parsing fails:
- raise a descriptive exception
- include the file path in the error message

### 10.2 Missing key errors
If required config keys are missing:
- raise a clear validation error
- identify which key path is absent

### 10.3 Serialization errors
If a given object cannot be serialized to JSON:
- either convert it safely
- or raise a descriptive error rather than silently truncating data

---

## 11) What this module should not do

To keep the design clean and faithful to the paper:

- do not perform data loading
- do not split datasets
- do not preprocess data
- do not build models
- do not train models
- do not compute metrics
- do not tune hyperparameters

It should only handle:
- config loading/validation
- JSON/pickle persistence

---

## 12) Recommended implementation priorities

Order of implementation for this module:

1. Implement `load_config(path)`
2. Add schema validation for required keys
3. Implement `save_json(obj, path)`
4. Implement `save_pickle(obj, path)`
5. Implement `load_pickle(path)`
6. Add helper functions for path normalization and safe serialization

---

## 13) Paper alignment summary

This module supports the reproduction of the paper by ensuring:

- the exact `config.yaml` governs experiment behavior
- all hyperparameters and dataset policies match the paper
- tuning/search spaces are read from configuration, not hardcoded
- evaluation settings such as 15 seeds and ensemble grouping are preserved
- all saved outputs are reproducible and traceable

In short, `utils/io.py` is the glue that guarantees the rest of the system follows the paper’s experimental protocol exactly.

[ANALYSIS] data/dataset_loader.py
============================================
# Logic Analysis: `data/dataset_loader.py`

This module is responsible for **loading raw benchmark datasets**, **identifying feature types**, and **producing the exact train/validation/test split objects** used by the rest of the pipeline. It must be designed to support the paper’s 11-tabular-benchmark setup and remain fully config-driven.

## 1) Role in the reproduction pipeline

`DatasetLoader` is the first data-specific component in the experiment flow:

1. Read `ExperimentConfig`
2. Load dataset from local storage or configured source
3. Map columns into:
   - numerical features
   - categorical features
   - target column
4. Reproduce the paper’s split protocol:
   - train
   - validation
   - test
5. Return a structured object that downstream preprocessing and training can consume

This module must not perform preprocessing itself; it only loads and splits raw data.

---

## 2) Inputs and outputs

### Inputs
The loader should use only configuration values and dataset metadata, especially:

- `config.dataset_name`
- `config.dataset_params` if present
- `config.seed` from `experiment.seed`
- dataset-specific batch or split-related settings if encoded in config
- file/path information from config or agreed conventions

### Outputs
`load_and_split()` should return a `DatasetSplits`-like structure as defined in the design, containing:

- `train_df`, `val_df`, `test_df`
- `y_train`, `y_val`, `y_test`
- `numerical_cols`
- `categorical_cols`

The returned data must preserve row alignment between feature tables and target arrays.

---

## 3) Datasets that must be supported

The paper uses 11 datasets:

- Gesture Phase Prediction (`GE`)
- Churn Modelling (`CH`)
- California Housing (`CA`)
- House 16H (`HO`)
- Adult (`AD`)
- Otto Group Product Classification (`OT`)
- Higgs Small (`HI`)
- Facebook Comments Volume (`FB`)
- Santander Customer Transaction Prediction (`SA`)
- Covertype (`CO`)
- Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K fold 1 (`MI`)

The loader must support all 11 via one unified interface.

---

## 4) Dataset-specific loading logic

### 4.1 General pattern
For most datasets, `load_raw()` should:

- locate the dataset in a known directory or via a configured path
- read into a `pandas.DataFrame`
- identify target column
- preserve original row order unless the splitting logic requires shuffling

The module should not invent URLs or download procedures beyond what is configured or conventionally expected from the benchmark codebase.

### 4.2 Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K
This dataset is special:

- The paper explicitly says it uses **Fold 1**
- Loading should be fold-aware
- The loader must support a file layout or dataset parameter that identifies the fold
- Since the paper treats it as a regression task, target handling should preserve numeric labels

The loader should not assume it can download or reconstruct this dataset automatically unless a path is explicitly configured.

### 4.3 Otto Group Product Classification
This dataset is special in preprocessing, but for loading:

- it has categorical labels/classes
- the loader must detect that it is a multiclass classification task
- the loader must expose numeric and categorical columns correctly
- the split protocol must still be consistent with the paper’s benchmark split strategy

### 4.4 Datasets with mixed feature types
Several datasets contain both numerical and categorical features:

- `CH`
- `AD`
- `OT`
- potentially others depending on raw source representation

The loader must infer or map these correctly based on dataset metadata or explicit configuration, not by guessing later in preprocessing.

---

## 5) Split protocol requirements

The paper states that datasets are split into:

- train
- validation
- test

and that validation is used for:

- hyperparameter tuning
- early stopping

test is used only for final evaluation.

### 5.1 Reproducibility requirement
The split logic must be deterministic given `config.seed` and dataset name.

### 5.2 Split source
The paper says it mostly follows Gorishniy et al. and mentions a split script in the supplementary material. Since the exact script is not provided here, the loader should:

- support a configurable split ratio or split specification if present in config
- support loading predefined split indices if the dataset files already contain them
- otherwise perform a deterministic split using `sklearn.model_selection`

### 5.3 Recommended behavior
The module should prioritize the following order:

1. Use predefined official split indices if available
2. Use explicit split settings from config if provided
3. Fall back to deterministic split generation using `config.seed`

This preserves reproducibility while remaining practical.

---

## 6) Feature-type identification

The loader must produce `numerical_cols` and `categorical_cols`.

### 6.1 Preferred method
If dataset metadata is known, feature types should be assigned from a dataset registry.

### 6.2 Fallback method
If metadata is incomplete, infer from dtypes:

- numeric dtypes → numerical
- object/category/string → categorical

But this fallback should be used carefully because some tabular datasets may encode categorical values as integers. For those, dataset-specific metadata is safer.

### 6.3 Important constraint
The downstream preprocessing module expects these lists to be correct and stable. The loader must not reorder columns arbitrarily.

---

## 7) Internal structure recommendation

Although code is not being written yet, the logic should be organized around a dataset registry inside `DatasetLoader`.

### Suggested registry contents
For each dataset name:

- file name / directory pattern
- target column name
- task type:
  - binary classification
  - multiclass classification
  - regression
- numerical columns
- categorical columns
- optional fold specification
- optional split source / ratio

This registry can be implemented as internal metadata, but it must remain consistent with the config and paper.

---

## 8) `load_raw()` logic

`load_raw()` should:

1. Resolve dataset name from config
2. Resolve data path from config or conventional location
3. Read dataset into a DataFrame
4. Normalize column names if needed
5. Attach or preserve target column
6. Return the raw DataFrame ready for splitting

### Notes
- It should not alter values except for minimal parsing
- It should not preprocess missing values globally unless a dataset requires a parsing step
- It should avoid any random operations

---

## 9) `split_data(df)` logic

`split_data(df)` should:

1. Determine feature/target column identities
2. Extract `X` and `y`
3. Use dataset-specific split strategy
4. Return train/val/test partitions

### 9.1 Deterministic split
If using `sklearn.model_selection`, the split should be deterministic by seeding with `config.seed`.

### 9.2 Validation split
The paper uses validation for tuning and early stopping, so the loader should produce a separate validation partition, not cross-validation folds, unless the dataset’s official protocol demands otherwise.

### 9.3 Class balance / stratification
For classification datasets, the split should be stratified if the benchmark protocol or the loaded split script supports it. If not explicitly available, this should be documented as a fallback decision in implementation notes.

### 9.4 Regression
For regression datasets:
- use ordinary deterministic split
- do not stratify
- keep target values unchanged here; standardization happens later in preprocessing

---

## 10) `load_and_split()` logic

This should be the main convenience entrypoint.

### Sequence
1. Call `load_raw()`
2. Call `split_data(df)`
3. Return the split object with:
   - dataframes
   - target arrays
   - feature type lists

This method should be the one used by `main.py`.

---

## 11) Config dependencies from `config.yaml`

The loader must reference configuration values explicitly available in `config.yaml`:

- `experiment.seed`
- `data.preprocessing.numerical.default`
- `data.preprocessing.numerical.otto_group_product_classification`
- `data.preprocessing.categorical.default`
- `training.batch_size.*` only if the loader needs dataset identification metadata, but not for loading itself
- `notes.implementation_constraints` for the rule that Otto must not receive feature preprocessing later

The loader should **not** invent split ratios or paths unless they are supplied elsewhere in config or inferred from a controlled local dataset layout.

---

## 12) Error handling requirements

The loader should fail clearly if:

- dataset name is unknown
- required file is missing
- target column is absent
- numerical/categorical schema cannot be determined
- fold-based data is requested but the fold file is missing

Useful errors should mention the dataset name and expected file/pattern.

---

## 13) Reproducibility considerations

Because the paper reports averages over multiple seeds and tuned hyperparameters, this loader must be fully deterministic for a given seed. That means:

- any random split must use the configured seed
- no hidden shuffling unless intentional
- if split indices are saved, they should be reused exactly

This is especially important for fair comparisons across embedding methods.

---

## 14) Interface expectations from the design

This module must conform to the existing design and should only expose the methods already listed:

- `__init__(config)`
- `load_raw()`
- `split_data(df)`
- `load_and_split()`

Do not add public methods beyond this interface.

---

## 15) Implementation priority within this file

The logic should be implemented in this order:

1. Dataset registry / metadata mapping
2. Raw file resolution
3. Column type detection
4. Deterministic split generation
5. Fold-aware support for Microsoft
6. Final `load_and_split()` wrapper

This ensures the rest of the pipeline can rely on a stable dataset object from the start.

---

## 16) Summary of key requirements

- Support all 11 datasets from the paper
- Keep splitting deterministic and config-driven
- Preserve exact train/val/test separation
- Identify numerical and categorical columns correctly
- Handle Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K fold loading
- Avoid preprocessing here; only load and split
- Use only the interfaces defined in the design
- Reference `config.yaml` values directly and do not invent hidden settings

[ANALYSIS] data/preprocessing.py
============================================
## Logic Analysis: `data/preprocessing.py`

This module is responsible for reproducing the paper’s preprocessing protocol exactly and consistently across all model families. It must be aligned with:

- the paper’s preprocessing rules,
- the shared design for `Preprocessor` and `PreprocessedData`,
- and the configuration values in `config.yaml`.

It is a shared dependency for:

- deep models: MLP, ResNet, Transformer-L
- GBDT baselines: CatBoost, XGBoost
- tuning and final evaluation

Because the paper emphasizes fair comparison, this module must ensure that all downstream components see the same transformed data for a given dataset and experimental setting.

---

### 1) Purpose and responsibilities

`data/preprocessing.py` must implement:

- `Preprocessor.fit(splits)`
- `Preprocessor.transform(splits)`
- `Preprocessor.fit_transform(splits)`

Its main responsibilities are:

1. **Apply the correct numerical preprocessing**
   - default: `quantile_transform`
   - exception: `otto_group_product_classification -> none`

2. **Apply categorical encoding**
   - default for non-CatBoost algorithms: `one_hot`
   - CatBoost must still be able to use raw categorical features, so the module should preserve categorical data in a usable form, even if one-hot is not applied for that path

3. **Standardize regression targets**
   - enabled by config:
     - `data.target.regression_standardization: true`

4. **Fit preprocessing only on training data**
   - transform validation/test using fitted transformers from training only
   - never fit on validation/test data

5. **Return a unified `PreprocessedData` container**
   - with train/val/test splits
   - in a format accepted by both training and baseline pipelines

---

### 2) Required configuration values to honor

All decisions must be driven by `config.yaml`; no values should be invented.

Relevant config keys:

- `data.preprocessing.numerical.default = quantile_transform`
- `data.preprocessing.numerical.otto_group_product_classification = none`
- `data.preprocessing.categorical.default = one_hot`
- `data.target.regression_standardization = true`

Training/evaluation constraints that depend on preprocessing:

- `evaluation.metrics.classification = accuracy`
- `evaluation.metrics.regression = rmse`
- `training.loss.classification = cross_entropy`
- `training.loss.regression = mse`

The preprocessor should not embed any training logic, but it must output labels in a shape and dtype compatible with those loss functions.

---

### 3) Interface and data flow

The design specifies:

- Input: `DatasetSplits`
- Output: `PreprocessedData`

The module must use the exact data structures from the design and should not introduce incompatible alternatives.

#### Expected input fields from `DatasetSplits`

- `train_df`, `val_df`, `test_df`
- `y_train`, `y_val`, `y_test`
- `numerical_cols`
- `categorical_cols`

The preprocessor should assume that dataset loading has already identified numerical and categorical columns.

#### Expected output fields in `PreprocessedData`

- `x_train_num`, `x_val_num`, `x_test_num`
- `x_train_cat`, `x_val_cat`, `x_test_cat`
- `y_train`, `y_val`, `y_test`
- `num_features`, `cat_features`
- `feature_metadata`

The output should be usable by:

- the model factory for deep learning models,
- the GBDT baseline runner,
- and metrics evaluation.

---

### 4) Preprocessing policy by feature type

## 4.1 Numerical features

### Default policy
From config and paper:

- use **quantile transformation** for all datasets except Otto Group Product Classification

### Otto exception
- for `otto_group_product_classification`, do **not** apply numerical preprocessing
- this means numerical features should be passed through unchanged, apart from any required dtype conversion

### Fit behavior
The quantile transformer must be fit on the **training numerical features only**.

### Transform behavior
The fitted transformer is then applied to:
- training
- validation
- test

### Implementation notes
Use `sklearn.preprocessing.QuantileTransformer` or the closest equivalent consistent with the paper’s description.

Important constraints:
- fit only on training data
- preserve deterministic behavior through the global seed utilities
- ensure outputs are finite and consistent in shape

### Shape handling
The transformed numerical data should remain 2D:
- shape: `[n_samples, n_num_features]`

This is critical because all downstream embedding modules expect a dense numeric matrix.

---

## 4.2 Categorical features

### Configured default
- `data.preprocessing.categorical.default = one_hot`

### Paper rule
For all non-CatBoost algorithms, categorical features are one-hot encoded.

### Preprocessor responsibility
The preprocessor should support categorical one-hot encoding as the default output path.

However, because CatBoost uses raw categorical support, the pipeline should preserve the ability to access the unencoded categorical values or separate a CatBoost-compatible path from the one-hot path.

### Practical interpretation for this module
The safest design, consistent with the shared preprocessing goal, is:

- fit a `OneHotEncoder` on training categorical columns
- transform val/test with the same encoder
- produce encoded categorical matrices for deep models and non-CatBoost baselines

But the module must also preserve enough metadata to allow CatBoost to bypass one-hot encoding if the baseline runner needs raw categorical columns. Since the design does not define a separate raw-cat output field, this should be handled through `feature_metadata` and/or by storing the original dataframe columns in a way the baseline runner can access before preprocessing, if required by the broader pipeline.

Because the module design is fixed, do not add public interfaces beyond `fit`, `transform`, and `fit_transform`. Any extra compatibility should be achieved internally through the returned container and metadata.

### Important consistency requirement
Categorical encoding must be fit using training data only.

---

### 5) Regression target standardization

The config explicitly says:

- `data.target.regression_standardization: true`

The paper states regression targets are standardized for all algorithms.

Therefore:

- if task type is regression, standardize `y_train`
- apply the same transformation to `y_val` and `y_test`
- store the fitted target scaler from training only

This affects datasets such as:
- California Housing
- House 16H
- Facebook Comments Volume
- Covertype? No, Covertype is classification
- Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K, which the paper treats as regression

### Classification targets
For classification:
- do not standardize
- preserve label encoding as required by the trainer/loss
- ensure labels are in the expected integer class format

---

### 6) Dataset-specific preprocessing behavior

The module must respect dataset differences in `config.yaml` and paper text.

## 6.1 Otto Group Product Classification
- numerical preprocessing: `none`
- categorical features still exist in this dataset
- deep models and non-CatBoost algorithms still require a consistent representation

## 6.2 All other datasets
- numerical preprocessing: `quantile_transform`
- categorical features: one-hot by default for non-CatBoost methods

## 6.3 Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K
- task is treated as regression in the paper
- target standardization applies
- categorical handling depends on the dataset definition from the loader, but preprocessing should not assume a special exception unless the dataset loader provides one

---

### 7) Fit/transform contract

The module must obey standard preprocessing semantics.

## 7.1 `fit(splits)`
Should:

- inspect `splits.numerical_cols` and `splits.categorical_cols`
- fit numerical transformer if applicable
- fit categorical encoder if categorical columns exist
- fit target scaler if regression standardization is enabled
- store all fitted transformers internally for later `transform`

It should not mutate the input splits in-place.

## 7.2 `transform(splits)`
Should:

- apply stored numerical transformer to val/test/train
- apply stored categorical encoder to val/test/train
- apply target transformation if regression
- produce a `PreprocessedData` object
- convert outputs into the expected container types

## 7.3 `fit_transform(splits)`
Should:
- call `fit`
- then `transform`
- return `PreprocessedData`

This method is especially useful for the main experiment entrypoint.

---

### 8) Data representation and typing

The module should be strict about the output structure, because the rest of the system expects stable shapes and dtypes.

## 8.1 Numerical arrays
- should be stored as dense arrays/tensors
- `float32` is the safest default for PyTorch compatibility

## 8.2 Categorical arrays
Possible representations depend on whether one-hot encoding is applied:

- if one-hot encoded: dense `float32` or sparse-compatible arrays converted to dense if the downstream model expects it
- if preserved as raw categories: this should only happen if the broader pipeline explicitly supports it, but the design currently emphasizes one-hot for non-CatBoost

Given the design, returning one-hot encoded categorical tensors is the safer default.

## 8.3 Targets
- regression: `float32`
- classification: integer class labels, usually `int64` for PyTorch cross-entropy

## 8.4 Container metadata
`feature_metadata` should record:
- original numerical feature names
- original categorical feature names
- output dimensionality after preprocessing
- fitted transformer types
- whether numerical preprocessing was applied
- whether regression targets were standardized

This metadata is necessary for downstream model construction and reproducibility logging.

---

### 9) Compatibility with the model pipeline

This module must produce data that matches the assumptions of:

## 9.1 Embedding modules
- numerical tensors should be 2D dense matrices
- embeddings operate on per-feature scalar inputs, so numerical preprocessing must not collapse feature structure

## 9.2 MLP/ResNet
- likely consume concatenated numerical and categorical inputs
- output from `PreprocessedData` must be compatible with concatenation logic

## 9.3 Transformer-L
- expects token-like feature representations
- numerical preprocessing must preserve feature-wise columns

## 9.4 GBDT baselines
- CatBoost should be able to use categorical variables appropriately
- XGBoost should receive the standardized, encoded version consistent with the paper’s fair-comparison protocol

---

### 10) Important reproducibility constraints

The paper and config emphasize fair comparison and deterministic behavior.

This module must ensure:

1. **Training-only fitting**
   - no leakage from val/test

2. **Stable column ordering**
   - the order of numerical and categorical columns must be preserved consistently across splits

3. **Deterministic transforms**
   - transformer behavior should be reproducible given the global seed

4. **No hidden preprocessing differences across model families**
   - the same dataset must use the same preprocessing policy for all deep models, as stated in `notes.implementation_constraints`

5. **Otto exception must be honored exactly**
   - this is the only explicit numerical-preprocessing exception in config

---

### 11) Recommended internal structure

While the public interface is fixed, internally the module should separate concerns into:

- numerical transformer setup
- categorical encoder setup
- target scaler setup
- split transformation helpers
- packaging into `PreprocessedData`

This separation will make it easier to keep the logic faithful to the paper and maintainable.

---

### 12) Edge cases to handle

The implementation should be robust to:

- datasets with no categorical columns
- datasets with no numerical columns
- constant-valued features
- missing bins or empty quantiles in preprocessing
- regression targets with one-dimensional shape
- classification labels needing integer conversion
- sparse one-hot outputs that must be converted for PyTorch compatibility

For one-hot encoding, be careful with:
- unseen categories in validation/test
- set encoder behavior to ignore unknowns if necessary, while still preserving training-defined feature spaces

---

### 13) What must not be changed

Per the design constraints:

- do **not** change `Preprocessor` public API
- do **not** invent new public methods
- do **not** change the `PreprocessedData` interface
- do **not** introduce preprocessing policies not supported by the paper/config
- do **not** apply feature preprocessing to Otto numerical features
- do **not** fit preprocessing on validation/test data

---

### 14) Summary of module behavior

In compact form, `data/preprocessing.py` should implement:

- **Numerical preprocessing**
  - quantile transform on all datasets except Otto
  - none for Otto

- **Categorical preprocessing**
  - one-hot encoding by default for non-CatBoost use
  - preserve enough structure for fair downstream comparison

- **Target preprocessing**
  - standardize regression targets when enabled

- **Output**
  - a `PreprocessedData` object containing train/val/test arrays/tensors and metadata

This module is the bridge between raw dataset splits and all subsequent modeling stages, so correctness here is essential for reproducing the paper’s results faithfully.
