 38%|███▊      | 5/13 [01:39<02:57, 22.17s/it] 46%|████▌     | 6/13 [02:04<02:42, 23.23s/it] 54%|█████▍    | 7/13 [02:27<02:19, 23.24s/it] 62%|██████▏   | 8/13 [02:45<01:47, 21.54s/it]# Logic Analysis: `models/embeddings.py`

This module is the core methodological component of the reproduction. It must implement the numerical-feature embedding mechanisms used throughout the paper, while respecting the paper’s key constraint that **each numerical feature is embedded independently** and **parameters are never shared across features**.

The file must define:

- `BaseEmbedding`
- `LinearEmbedding`
- `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding`
- `PeriodicEmbedding`
- `EmbeddingFactory`

It must also support the model composition patterns used in the experiments, especially:

- `MLP-L`, `MLP-LR`, `MLP-LRLR`
- `MLP-Q`, `MLP-Q-LR`, `MLP-Q-LRLR`
- `MLP-T`, `MLP-T-LR`, `MLP-T-LRLR`
- `MLP-P`, `MLP-PL`, `MLP-PLR`
- analogous variants for `ResNet` and `Transformer-L`

Below is the logic analysis for how to implement this module correctly and reproducibly.

---

## 1) Core design constraints from the paper

### 1.1 Feature-wise independent embeddings
The paper explicitly states:

- each numerical feature `x_i` is embedded independently
- all features use the same functional form
- parameters are not shared between features

This means the implementation must avoid:
- any layer that mixes numerical features before embedding
- any shared embedding table or shared binning parameters across features

Each feature needs its own embedding path, even if the module class is the same.

### 1.2 Output usage depends on backbone
The paper distinguishes how embeddings are consumed:

- **MLP/ResNet-like backbones**: concatenate all feature embeddings into one flat vector
- **Transformer-like backbones**: keep embeddings as a token sequence

So `embeddings.py` must expose output in a way that downstream backbones can use consistently. The module should not decide final model usage; it should only produce:
- per-feature embeddings
- either stacked token embeddings or flattened vectors depending on the wrapper’s request

Because `tabular_model.py` is responsible for backbone composition, this module should focus on embedding generation and return a consistent tensor shape that the wrapper can consume.

### 1.3 Same embedding family across all features
The paper says:
- no feature-specific functional form variation
- no sharing of parameters between feature embeddings

So for example, PLE should be the same type for every feature, but each feature can have its own bins.

---

## 2) Expected interfaces and internal responsibilities

Although the exact method signatures are not shown in the paper, the module should support the data flow implied by the design:

### `BaseEmbedding`
A lightweight abstract base class defining the common interface for numerical embeddings.

Responsibilities:
- store metadata like `num_features`, `output_dim`
- define `forward(x)` contract
- optionally define utility methods for shape validation

### `LinearEmbedding`
Implements the “bias-free linear layer” embedding module used in the paper.

Responsibilities:
- apply a separate linear transform to each scalar feature
- no bias in the linear mapping
- each feature should have its own parameters unless the wrapper intentionally uses a shared projection per feature value representation
- output embedding dimension should be tunable from config/search space

Important: the paper says `Linear` denotes a **bias-free linear layer**. This should be interpreted literally.

### `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding`
Implements PLE, including both:
- quantile-based binning
- target-aware binning

Responsibilities:
- build per-feature bins using training data only
- encode each scalar value into a piecewise-linear vector of length `T_i`
- optionally map the PLE vector to an embedding dimension using a bias-free linear layer, especially for Transformer-style models
- preserve feature independence

### `PeriodicEmbedding`
Implements the periodic activation embedding described in the paper.

Responsibilities:
- per-feature periodic transform
- trainable coefficients initialized from `N(0, sigma)`
- support tunable `sigma` and `k`
- do not mix features during periodic embedding
- output should be compatible with both flat and token-style downstream consumption

### `EmbeddingFactory`
Maps model names and config settings to the correct embedding class and variant.

Responsibilities:
- interpret names like `L`, `Q`, `T`, `P`, `PL`, `PLR`
- create the correct embedding module according to backbone and embedding suffix
- enforce dataset-specific or model-specific parameter choices coming from `config.yaml`

---

## 3) Configuration-driven behavior

This module must strictly reference `config.yaml` and not invent values.

### Relevant config keys
From the provided config:

#### Hyperparameter tuning
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.linear_embedding_output_dim: [1, 128]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_quantiles: [2, 256]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.max_leaves: [2, 256]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.min_items_per_leaf: [1, 128]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.min_information_gain: [1.0e-09, 0.01]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.periodic_k: [1, 128]`

#### Model settings
- MLP layer search ranges
- ResNet layer search ranges
- Transformer-specific hyperparameter ranges

#### Training
- `training.optimizer = adamw`
- `training.early_stopping.patience = 16`
- `training.max_epochs = 100`
- `training.lr_schedule = none`

#### Data preprocessing
- numerical default = quantile transform
- Otto = none
- categorical default = one_hot

### Important implications for embeddings
- `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding` should not assume a fixed number of bins; this must be tunable and constructed from config-driven search spaces.
- `PeriodicEmbedding` must use `k` from the search space.
- `LinearEmbedding` output dimension must be tunable from the corresponding search space.
- For PLE target-aware bins, the three tree parameters are the only tunable ones explicitly provided.
- No extra hidden defaults should be introduced unless required for shape compatibility by the paper’s architecture.

---

## 4) `LinearEmbedding` logic

### 4.1 What it represents
The paper’s `Linear` module is a bias-free linear projection. It is used as:
- a simple numerical feature embedding baseline
- a post-PLE projection for Transformer-like models
- a cheap way to add expressivity

### 4.2 Functional behavior
For each numerical feature scalar `x_i`, apply a learned linear transform to map it to a dense vector.

Implementation logic:
- input shape: `[batch_size, num_features]`
- per-feature independent scalar-to-vector projection
- output shape:
  - for MLP/ResNet: flattened or concatenated representation
  - for Transformer: one token per feature, each token vector size `output_dim`

### 4.3 Important constraints
- no bias term
- no activation inside the embedding itself
- if used as a “post-PLE” layer, it must accept PLE vectors as input
- the paper notes that MLP-L can be fused with the first backbone linear layer after training; this means the module should be a plain linear mapping, not a composite block

### 4.4 Practical shape considerations
There are two possible interpretation layers:
1. **Scalar-to-vector per feature**
2. **Vector-to-vector projection when applied after PLE**

The module should support both use cases through consistent internal design, but the public interface should remain unified and be used by `EmbeddingFactory` according to model variant.

---

## 5) `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding` logic

This is the most important and nuanced part of the module.

## 5.1 PLE concept
PLE encodes a scalar value into a vector of length `T` representing its relative location among bins.

For a value `x` and bin boundaries:
- values below a bin contribute `0`
- values above a bin contribute `1`
- values inside the active bin are interpolated linearly

This yields an ordered, continuous, piecewise-linear representation.

## 5.2 Per-feature bin construction
The paper is explicit that:
- bins are feature-specific
- bins are built independently
- no parameter sharing across features

So the class must store:
- one bin boundary set per feature
- one encoding configuration per feature

This means `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding` needs internal structures like:
- list of boundaries per feature
- one encoder per feature
- possibly one post-encoding linear transform per feature if used in Transformer mode

## 5.3 Quantile-based bins
This corresponds to `PLE-q`.

According to the paper:
- compute empirical quantiles from the **training set only**
- use the same number of quantiles for all features
- remove trivial zero-size bins

From config:
- `ple_quantiles: [2, 256]`

Logic:
1. during `fit`, receive training numerical matrix
2. for each feature:
   - compute quantile boundaries on training values
   - discard duplicate boundaries
   - store boundaries
3. during `forward`, convert each scalar into PLE vector using these boundaries

### Key reproducibility point
Quantiles must be learned only from training data to avoid leakage.

## 5.4 Target-aware bins
This corresponds to `PLE-t`.

According to the paper:
- construct bins using a supervised decision-tree-like discretization
- equivalent in spirit to C4.5 discretization
- the bins are obtained by recursively splitting a single feature using target information
- store `b_0 = min(x)` and `b_T = max(x)`

From config:
- `max_leaves`
- `min_items_per_leaf`
- `min_information_gain`

Logic:
1. for each feature independently:
   - fit a 1D decision tree on feature values and target
   - use the tree’s leaf partition as bins
2. store split boundaries
3. derive bins from these boundaries
4. apply PLE encoding using those bins

### Important implementation note
The tree is not a full multivariate tree. It is a **per-feature 1D discretizer**.

## 5.5 PLE encoding formula
The paper’s encoding is piecewise linear over bins.

Implementation logic for a single scalar `x`:
- identify the active bin interval
- produce a vector of bin activations
- previous bins are 1
- future bins are 0
- active bin contains the linear interpolation

Edge cases:
- `x < b_0`
- `x >= b_T`
- exact boundary values

The paper notes that these edge cases are covered by the formula and may produce values slightly outside the `[0,1]` interval at the extreme ends. The implementation should preserve the paper’s behavior rather than clipping unless necessary for numerical stability.

## 5.6 PLE for Transformer vs MLP/ResNet
The paper explicitly says:
- for MLP-like models, PLE representations are concatenated directly
- for Transformer-like models, an additional linear layer is needed after PLE

Therefore, `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding` should support a mode that returns:
- raw PLE vector
- or PLE followed by a linear projection

This is important because:
- the Transformer is permutation-invariant over token order
- the post-PL layer reintroduces feature identity

## 5.7 Avoiding leakage and mismatch
The class must separate:
- fitting bins
- encoding values
- optional projection layer

That ensures:
- bins are derived only from training split
- the same bins are used for validation/test
- feature indices remain independent

---

## 6) `PeriodicEmbedding` logic

This module adapts periodic activation functions to tabular numerical features.

## 6.1 Paper-specific constraints
The paper differs from original Fourier-feature style methods in two critical ways:

1. features are embedded **independently**
2. pre-activation coefficients are **trainable**, not fixed

So the implementation should not mix features before the periodic transform.

## 6.2 Tunable parameters
From the paper and config:
- `sigma` controls initialization scale
- `k` controls periodic dimensionality / number of components

From config:
- `periodic_k: [1, 128]`

The exact sigma search space is not listed in config, so the code should:
- accept sigma from configuration if provided
- otherwise use a clearly injected parameter from tuning logic
- never hardcode an undocumented sigma range

## 6.3 Initialization
The paper states:
- coefficients are initialized from `N(0, sigma)`

So each feature should have its own coefficient tensor initialized with that distribution.

## 6.4 Functional behavior
For each scalar feature:
- compute periodic features using trainable coefficients
- maintain feature independence
- output an embedding vector of dimension controlled by `k` or by the chosen implementation layout

The module should be compatible with:
- base periodic embeddings
- periodic + linear
- periodic + linear + ReLU

## 6.5 Practical implementation constraint
Because the exact equation is not fully visible in the provided paper text, the implementation should be designed so that:
- the periodic transform is encapsulated cleanly
- the coefficient initialization and `k` are configurable
- the downstream wrapper can extend it with linear/ReLU layers without changing periodic logic

This keeps the code faithful while leaving room to align with the exact source implementation if later verified.

---

## 7) `EmbeddingFactory` logic

The factory must map model naming conventions to module construction.

## 7.1 Naming patterns to support
The paper uses the `Backbone-Embedding` naming convention.

Examples:
- `MLP`
- `MLP-L`
- `MLP-LR`
- `MLP-LRLR`
- `MLP-Q`
- `MLP-Q-LR`
- `MLP-Q-LRLR`
- `MLP-T`
- `MLP-T-LR`
- `MLP-T-LRLR`
- `MLP-P`
- `MLP-PL`
- `MLP-PLR`

And similarly for ResNet / Transformer.

## 7.2 Factory responsibilities
The factory should:
- parse the suffix
- instantiate the appropriate embedding class
- configure it based on backbone type
- pass along tuned hyperparameters
- ensure the correct output dimensions for the selected backbone

## 7.3 Backbone-specific differences
The factory should know:
- MLP/ResNet usually consume concatenated feature embeddings
- Transformer consumes token embeddings directly
- PLE and periodic modules may need an extra linear projection in transformer mode

This means the factory should not merely choose the embedding family; it should also set the correct post-processing mode.

## 7.4 Config use
The factory must pull values only from:
- `config.models.*`
- `config.hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.*`
- `config.training.*`
- `config.data.preprocessing.*`

It should not invent default hyperparameters beyond what is necessary for tensor shape compatibility, and if a value is missing from config, it should be surfaced as an explicit requirement rather than silently chosen.

---

## 8) Shape and tensor contract

This module and the downstream model wrappers must agree on tensor shapes.

## 8.1 Input expectations
The preprocessing module will likely provide:
- `x_num`: tensor of shape `[batch_size, num_numeric_features]`
- potentially `x_cat`: handled elsewhere

This module should focus on `x_num`.

## 8.2 Output expectations
Depending on the embedding type and backbone, output should be either:

- `[batch_size, num_features * emb_dim]` for concatenated tabular MLP/ResNet usage
- `[batch_size, num_features, emb_dim]` for Transformer token usage

The code should clearly separate these two cases to avoid silent shape bugs.

## 8.3 Handling missing/categorical features
This module should not embed categorical features. Those are handled elsewhere per the design.
If categorical data are passed into this module, that would be an interface error.

---

## 9) Reproducibility requirements

The paper uses:
- 15 random seeds per tuned configuration
- early stopping with patience 16
- no learning rate schedule

Although these are mostly training-level concerns, embedding modules can still affect reproducibility in two ways:

### 9.1 Deterministic bin fitting
Quantile binning and tree-based binning must be deterministic given the seed and training data.

### 9.2 Deterministic parameter initialization
PeriodicEmbedding and LinearEmbedding weights must be initialized in a seed-controlled way.

The module itself should not call random generators outside the global seed context established by `utils/reproducibility.py`.

---

## 10) Compatibility with model variants

This module must support all embedding families in the paper’s experiments.

### 10.1 Linear family
- `L`
- `LR`
- `LRLR`

This means the output of `LinearEmbedding` should be composable with additional layers outside the module if needed, or the factory should build the composite embedding stack.

### 10.2 PLE family
- `Q`
- `T`
- and with `-L`, `-LR`, `-LRLR`

The module should provide:
- PLE encoding
- optional linear projection
- optional activation stack if the factory composes it internally

### 10.3 Periodic family
- `P`
- `PL`
- `PLR`

The periodic module should be able to stand alone or be composed with post-layers.

---

## 11) Validation and safety checks

To make the module robust, it should validate:

1. **number of features**
   - the fitted bin list matches input feature count

2. **training-only fitting**
   - PLE bin construction must not be used before fitting

3. **finite boundaries**
   - bin boundaries must be finite and sorted

4. **non-empty bins**
   - remove duplicate or zero-width bins, as the paper states for quantile PLE

5. **shape consistency**
   - output shape must match backbone expectations

6. **task-independent behavior**
   - embedding modules should not depend on classification/regression logic

---

## 12) Implementation order recommendation for this file

To reduce risk, implement in this order:

1. `BaseEmbedding`
2. `LinearEmbedding`
3. `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding` with quantile bins first
4. target-aware PLE bins
5. `PeriodicEmbedding`
6. `EmbeddingFactory`
7. optional helper methods for feature-wise encoding and shape conversion

This order follows the paper’s conceptual structure and makes debugging easier.

---

## 13) Most important paper-faithful behaviors to preserve

If only a few details are treated as non-negotiable, they are these:

- embeddings are **feature-wise independent**
- no feature-parameter sharing
- quantile bins computed from **training data only**
- target-aware bins built **per feature** using target information
- PLE for transformer needs a **linear projection after PLE**
- periodic embeddings use **trainable coefficients**
- periodic initialization uses `N(0, sigma)`
- `Linear` means **bias-free linear layer**
- all hyperparameters must come from `config.yaml` or explicitly tuned search spaces

---

## 14) Final module-level conclusion

`models/embeddings.py` should be treated as the methodological heart of the reproduction. Its role is not just to “embed numbers,” but to faithfully encode the paper’s main hypothesis: **how numerical values are represented can strongly affect tabular DL performance**.

Therefore, the implementation must be:
- modular
- feature-wise independent
- config-driven
- compatible with multiple backbones
- faithful to the paper’s binning and periodic-embedding logic

If you want, I can next provide the same level of logic analysis for `models/backbones.py` or turn this into a precise implementation blueprint with class-by-class method responsibilities.

[ANALYSIS] models/backbones.py
============================================
## Logic Analysis: `models/backbones.py`

This module must implement the three backbone families used throughout the paper:

- `MLPBackbone`
- `ResNetBackbone`
- `TransformerBackbone`

It must be compatible with the embedding interface defined in `models/embeddings.py` and with the model composition logic in `models/tabular_model.py`.

The paper’s methodology and `config.yaml` constrain this module tightly:
- backbones must accept either:
  - a single flattened tensor for MLP-like models, or
  - a sequence of feature tokens for Transformer-style models
- hyperparameters must align with the search spaces in `config.yaml`
- no learning rate schedule or other hidden training behavior belongs here
- the backbone classes should be purely architectural components, not training logic

---

# 1) Role of this file in the system

`models/backbones.py` defines the reusable neural network cores for tabular prediction:

1. **MLPBackbone**
   - used for MLP-based models
   - receives a single flat vector, typically the concatenation of embedded numerical features and possibly categorical features already encoded upstream
   - outputs a prediction-ready latent representation or final logits/regression output, depending on composition design

2. **ResNetBackbone**
   - tabular residual network variant from the Gorishniy et al. benchmark family
   - same input style as MLP: flat vector
   - uses residual blocks with dropout and a multiplicative hidden expansion factor

3. **TransformerBackbone**
   - FT-Transformer style backbone
   - receives tokenized feature embeddings
   - processes them with self-attention across features
   - outputs a pooled representation for the head

This file should not:
- preprocess raw data
- perform embedding binning
- handle target scaling
- implement training loops
- manage metrics or tuning

Those responsibilities are elsewhere.

---

# 2) Interface expectations from the design

The design explicitly states:

- `MLPBackbone`
  - `__init__(input_dim: int, hidden_dim: int, num_layers: int, dropout: float)`
  - `forward(x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor`

- `ResNetBackbone`
  - `__init__(input_dim: int, hidden_dim: int, num_layers: int, hidden_factor: float, dropout: float)`
  - `forward(x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor`

- `TransformerBackbone`
  - `__init__(num_tokens: int, emb_dim: int, n_layers: int, n_heads: int, ffn_factor: float, dropout: float)`
  - `forward(x: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor`

Important implementation constraint:
- do not add public methods beyond this design
- internal helper methods are fine if kept private

---

# 3) Input/output contract

## 3.1 MLPBackbone input contract
Expected input:
- `x`: shape `[batch_size, input_dim]`

This will usually be produced by `TabularModel` after concatenating per-feature numerical embeddings, or by a simple embedding stack that already flattens tokens.

Expected output:
- latent vector of shape `[batch_size, hidden_dim]` or final hidden representation
- the exact final prediction head is handled by `models/tabular_model.py`, so the backbone should generally return the final hidden representation, not task logits directly

Rationale:
- the paper composes backbones with heads
- the backbone should remain reusable across classification/regression tasks

## 3.2 ResNetBackbone input contract
Expected input:
- `x`: shape `[batch_size, input_dim]`

Expected output:
- latent vector shape `[batch_size, hidden_dim]` or same base width used by the head

The residual architecture should preserve a stable latent width through residual blocks. If the first projection changes dimensionality, it should do so once at the input stage.

## 3.3 TransformerBackbone input contract
Expected input:
- `x`: shape `[batch_size, num_tokens, emb_dim]`

This matches FT-Transformer-style token input:
- each numerical feature embedding is a token
- categorical tokens may also be included if the broader model supports them, but the paper’s focus is numerical embeddings
- token order matters only because a positional/feature-index mechanism is already represented in the embeddings or token structure

Expected output:
- pooled latent vector shape `[batch_size, emb_dim]` or compatible hidden width for the downstream head

The backbone should therefore:
- process tokens through transformer blocks
- pool across tokens into a single representation

---

# 4) Paper-faithful architectural logic

## 4.1 MLPBackbone
The paper compares MLP-like models with different numerical embeddings and shows that proper embeddings can make MLP competitive with Transformer-based models.

For faithful reproduction:
- use a straightforward feed-forward stack
- each layer should follow the common tabular benchmark style:
  - linear projection
  - activation
  - dropout
- the backbone should not introduce any extra feature mixing beyond standard dense layers

### Architecture logic
A typical hidden pipeline is:

1. input projection from `input_dim` to `hidden_dim`
2. repeat `num_layers - 1` hidden blocks
3. each hidden block:
   - linear
   - activation
   - dropout

The paper does not specify a novel MLP activation in this file; use the standard activation used by the benchmark codebase if that is already fixed elsewhere. Since the design does not expose an activation hyperparameter here, the module should follow the benchmark’s default MLP choice consistently.

### Important note
The paper’s model naming distinguishes embeddings from backbones:
- `MLP-LR` means an embedding module containing linear/relu layers before the MLP
- the MLP backbone itself should remain the same regardless of embedding type

So this backbone must be embedding-agnostic.

---

## 4.2 ResNetBackbone
The paper follows the tabular ResNet style from Gorishniy et al.

The residual architecture should:
- first map input features to a working hidden width
- then apply residual blocks with hidden expansion controlled by `hidden_factor`
- use dropout inside blocks as prescribed by the tuned hyperparameters

### Residual block logic
Each residual block should conceptually:
1. normalize or transform input representation
2. expand hidden width to `hidden_dim * hidden_factor`
3. apply nonlinearity
4. project back to `hidden_dim`
5. apply dropout
6. add residual connection

Because the design does not expose a separate normalization class in this file, the implementation should remain consistent with the benchmark style but stay within the allowed interface. If normalization is used, it should be internal to the module.

### Shape logic
- input tensor `[B, input_dim]`
- projected to `[B, hidden_dim]`
- residual blocks preserve `[B, hidden_dim]`

### Why this matters
The paper’s ResNet comparison is important because it shows embeddings improve not only MLPs but also a stronger tabular backbone. So the ResNet implementation must be stable and standard, not a modified experimental variant.

---

## 4.3 TransformerBackbone
This backbone is the most structurally constrained because the paper uses FT-Transformer-like behavior.

### Required behavior
- process feature tokens independently at input
- mix them with self-attention blocks
- use dropout values from config
- use feed-forward expansion with factor `ffn_factor`
- output a pooled representation over feature tokens

### Input structure
The backbone expects a token sequence:
- shape `[B, T, D]`
where:
- `B` = batch size
- `T` = number of tokens / features
- `D` = embedding dimension

### Output structure
Most likely one pooled vector:
- `[B, D]`

Pooling should be consistent with FT-Transformer convention:
- either a dedicated CLS token if the composition module provides it
- or a simple pooling strategy if the benchmark code does not use CLS

Since the design for this module only exposes `num_tokens`, `emb_dim`, `n_layers`, `n_heads`, `ffn_factor`, and `dropout`, the safer assumption is:
- the backbone itself should handle the standard FT-Transformer processing of tokens and return a single latent representation
- any final prediction head is separate in `models/tabular_model.py`

### Attention logic
Each layer should include:
- multi-head self-attention
- residual connection
- feed-forward network using `ffn_factor`
- dropout

The `num_tokens` argument is needed to support the token count in the module, but the forward pass should primarily infer batch dimensions from input.

### Note on numerical embeddings
The paper emphasizes that embeddings are computed independently per feature before mixing. Therefore:
- `TransformerBackbone` should not perform any per-feature embedding itself
- it only receives the already-embedded tokens from upstream modules

---

# 5) Hyperparameter alignment with `config.yaml`

The backbone module must respect the following config-driven ranges.

## 5.1 MLP
From `config.yaml`:
- `layers`: `[1, 16]`
- `layer_size`: `[1, 1024]`
- `dropout`: `[0.0, 0.5]`
- `weight_decay`: `[0.0, 1.0e-03]`

In this file:
- `layers`, `layer_size`, and `dropout` are architectural parameters
- `weight_decay` is handled by the optimizer/training logic, not here

So `MLPBackbone` should be parameterized to accept:
- the number of layers
- hidden size
- dropout
and should remain compatible with the ranges above.

## 5.2 ResNet
From `config.yaml`:
- `layers`: `[1, 8]`
- `layer_size`: `[32, 512]`
- `hidden_factor`: `[1.0, 4.0]`
- `hidden_dropout`: `[0.0, 0.5]`
- `residual_dropout`: `[0.0, 0.5]`
- `weight_decay`: `[0.0, 1.0e-03]`

This means:
- `hidden_dim` should match the tunable layer size
- `hidden_factor` must be used in the residual block expansion
- `dropout` in the design likely needs to cover the relevant dropout usage; if the implementation supports both hidden and residual dropout internally, it should keep them separate internally only if this does not violate the exposed interface
- `weight_decay` is not part of the backbone

Since the design signature only has one `dropout` argument, the implementation must map the configured dropout behavior into this single parameter consistently with the design, without adding new public constructor args.

## 5.3 Transformer
From `config.yaml`:
- `layers`:
  - `sa_co_mi`: `[2, 4]`
  - `other`: `[1, 4]`
- `embedding_size`:
  - `sa_co_mi`: `[192, 512]`
  - `other`: `[96, 512]`
- `residual_dropout`:
  - `sa_co_mi`: `0.0`
  - `other`: `[0.0, 0.2]`
- `attention_dropout`: `[0.0, 0.5]`
- `ffn_dropout`: `[0.0, 0.5]`
- `ffn_factor`: `[0.6666667, 2.6666667]`
- `weight_decay`: dataset-dependent

This implies `TransformerBackbone` must be compatible with:
- dataset-dependent number of layers
- dataset-dependent embedding dimension
- attention and FFN dropout
- FFN expansion ratio

The design exposes only one `dropout` argument, so the internal implementation must either:
- interpret it as the shared dropout value used by attention and/or FFN, or
- internally route it to the relevant dropout locations while keeping the public interface unchanged

You must not add extra constructor arguments beyond the design.

---

# 6) Token handling for TransformerBackbone

The paper’s transformer models are the FT-Transformer family. The key principles are:

1. each numerical feature is embedded independently
2. embeddings are treated as tokens
3. self-attention mixes tokens
4. the backbone itself does not create embeddings from raw scalars

This file should therefore assume that:
- upstream embedding code already produced feature tokens
- token order is stable and consistent across samples
- there is no need for additional manual feature mixing inside the backbone input stage

Potential output pooling strategies:
- CLS token if provided externally by model composition
- otherwise, pooled token summary

The exact pooling convention must be consistent with the rest of the codebase and the benchmark style, but it should not require public API changes here.

---

# 7) Internal class decomposition

Even though only three public classes are exposed, the implementation should likely use internal helper modules for clarity and correctness.

## Suggested internal helpers
These should remain private/internal to the file:

- `_MLPBlock`
- `_ResidualBlock`
- `_TransformerBlock`

These internal blocks can encapsulate:
- linear layers
- normalization
- activation
- dropout
- attention/FFN sublayers

This keeps the public API clean and aligned with the design.

---

# 8) Dependency relationships with other modules

## 8.1 Dependency on `models/embeddings.py`
`backbones.py` must be compatible with embedding outputs:
- `LinearEmbedding` and PLE variants may output flattened vectors for MLP/ResNet
- Transformer-compatible embeddings may output token sequences

Therefore, the backbone module must be shape-flexible:
- accept flat vectors for MLP/ResNet
- accept token tensors for Transformer

## 8.2 Dependency on `models/tabular_model.py`
`TabularModel` is responsible for composing embedding + backbone + head.

So `backbones.py` must not:
- decide task type
- create final output heads
- apply sigmoid/softmax
- compute loss

It should only emit latent features.

## 8.3 Dependency on `training/trainer.py`
Trainer will:
- call the model
- compute losses
- handle early stopping
- run optimization

The backbone must be deterministic under seed control and suitable for repeated instantiation across 15 seeds.

---

# 9) Reproducibility concerns

The paper requires careful reproducibility, so `backbones.py` should avoid hidden nondeterminism where possible.

Important points:
- use standard PyTorch modules only
- dropout should be the only intentional stochastic component
- initialization should follow PyTorch defaults unless benchmark code requires otherwise
- no internal randomness beyond layer initialization and dropout

Since `utils/reproducibility.py` will set seeds, the backbone should be deterministic given those seeds.

---

# 10) Shape and failure-mode checks

This module should be designed to fail loudly and clearly if the wrong tensor shape is passed.

## MLPBackbone
Expect:
- 2D tensor `[B, D]`

If a 3D tensor is passed accidentally, that indicates embedding composition mismatch.

## ResNetBackbone
Same expectation as MLP:
- 2D tensor `[B, D]`

## TransformerBackbone
Expect:
- 3D tensor `[B, T, D]`

If a flattened tensor is passed here, that indicates the model factory or embedding composition is incorrect.

These checks are important because embedding backbones are easy to wire incorrectly in tabular systems.

---

# 11) Alignment with the paper’s experimental intent

The paper’s experiments rely on comparing backbone behavior under different embedding schemes. Therefore this file should be intentionally conservative:

- MLP and ResNet should be standard, benchmark-style tabular networks
- Transformer should be FT-Transformer-like and token-based
- no experimental extras should be introduced here
- do not bake in dataset-specific logic
- do not bake in embedding-specific logic
- do not hard-code model names like `MLP-Q-LR`; that belongs in `models/tabular_model.py`

The purpose of this file is to provide the backbone primitives so the rest of the reproduction can mix and match embeddings exactly as in the paper.

---

# 12) Practical implementation order

Within `models/backbones.py`, implement in this order:

1. `MLPBackbone`
   - simplest
   - validates flat-vector pipeline

2. `ResNetBackbone`
   - uses shared conventions with MLP
   - residual block internal helper

3. `TransformerBackbone`
   - most complex
   - requires attention/FFN blocks and token pooling

This order reduces debugging complexity because the composition layer can first be validated with MLP before moving to residual and transformer pipelines.

---

# 13) Summary of key constraints

## Must follow
- public API exactly as designed
- flat input for MLP/ResNet
- token input for Transformer
- no public methods beyond design
- architecture compatible with paper’s benchmark setup
- hidden size / layer counts / dropout behavior consistent with `config.yaml`

## Must not do
- do not add preprocessing
- do not add loss/metric logic
- do not add tuning logic
- do not add dataset-specific code
- do not mix numerical features inside the backbone
- do not assume extra config values not present in `config.yaml`

---

# 14) Final implementation intent

`models/backbones.py` should be a clean, minimal, reproducible implementation of three standard tabular backbones, designed to plug into the paper’s embedding experiments:

- `MLPBackbone` for flattened embeddings
- `ResNetBackbone` for residual tabular modeling
- `TransformerBackbone` for FT-Transformer-style token processing

It should be written to support the paper’s core comparisons:
- vanilla embeddings vs PLE vs periodic embeddings
- MLP vs ResNet vs Transformer
- consistent performance evaluation across the 11 datasets and 15 seeds

[ANALYSIS] models/tabular_model.py
============================================
## Logic Analysis: `models/tabular_model.py`

This module is the composition layer that turns the paper’s abstract “Backbone-Embedding” naming convention into concrete PyTorch models. It must remain faithful to the paper’s feature-wise embedding framework and the configuration constraints from `config.yaml`.

---

### 1) Purpose of this module

`models/tabular_model.py` must:

1. **Compose**:
   - a numerical embedding module,
   - a backbone network,
   - a task-specific prediction head.

2. **Map model names deterministically** to the right architecture, e.g.:
   - `MLP`
   - `MLP-L`
   - `MLP-Q`
   - `MLP-Q-LR`
   - `MLP-PLR`
   - `ResNet-T-LR`
   - `Transformer-PLR`
   - etc.

3. **Handle task output correctly**:
   - classification: logits for classes
   - regression: single continuous output

4. **Route inputs consistently**:
   - `forward(x_num, x_cat=None)` should accept numerical tensors and optional categorical tensors.
   - Categorical handling should be compatible with the shared preprocessing strategy, but this module should not implement preprocessing itself.

---

### 2) Constraints from the paper that must be preserved

#### 2.1 Feature-wise independent numerical embeddings
The paper explicitly states that each numerical feature must be embedded independently and no parameters are shared across features. Therefore:

- The model factory must instantiate embedding modules that respect **per-feature separation**.
- The tabular model must not concatenate or mix raw scalar features before embedding, except where the embedding design explicitly requires a backbone-level mix afterward.

#### 2.2 Backbone-specific use of embeddings
From the paper:

- **MLP-like backbones**: embeddings are concatenated into a flat vector.
- **Transformer-like backbones**: embeddings are passed as token-like feature embeddings.

This means `TabularModel.forward()` must not assume one fixed embedding output format. It must trust the embedding module / backbone interface.

#### 2.3 PLE + Transformer requires a linear projection
The paper notes that PLE alone is not enough for Transformer-like models because the Transformer is permutation-invariant over token order. For Transformer variants with PLE, a linear layer after PLE is required.

So model construction must ensure:
- `Transformer-Q-L`, `Transformer-T-L`, and related variants include the correct post-PLE projection behavior as defined by the embedding module design.

#### 2.4 No invented hyperparameters
All architectural choices must come from `config.yaml` or the paper. In this file, the module should only interpret the model name and connect to the embedding/backbone constructors. It should not invent extra model-level options.

---

### 3) Inputs this module must expect

The class interfaces should align with the rest of the system:

- `x_num`: tensor of numerical features
- `x_cat`: optional tensor of categorical features

Given the shared design, this module should assume:
- numerical features are already preprocessed
- categorical features are already encoded for non-CatBoost models
- this module is not responsible for missing-value imputation or transformations

---

### 4) Core classes and responsibilities

---

## 4.1 `TabularModel`

This is the end-to-end model wrapper.

### Responsibilities
- Hold the embedding module
- Hold the backbone
- Hold the prediction head
- Implement `forward()`
- Provide a unified interface for training, validation, and inference

### Expected internal structure
Conceptually:

1. `embedding(x_num, x_cat)` or numerical-only embedding pipeline
2. backbone processing
3. prediction head

The exact behavior depends on the backbone type.

---

### 4.2 `ModelFactory`

This is the architecture resolver.

### Responsibilities
- Interpret `config.model_name`
- Instantiate the correct embedding module
- Instantiate the correct backbone
- Determine prediction head shape from `task_type`
- Return a `TabularModel`

The factory is where the model naming convention is mapped to actual components.

---

### 5) Model name parsing logic

The model names follow the paper’s `Backbone-Embedding` pattern.

Examples:
- `MLP`
- `MLP-L`
- `MLP-LR`
- `MLP-Q`
- `MLP-Q-LR`
- `MLP-T-LR`
- `ResNet-PLR`
- `Transformer-PLR`

The module must interpret these names by splitting them into:
- backbone prefix
- embedding suffix / composition suffix

### Backbone identifiers
The factory should recognize:
- `MLP`
- `ResNet`
- `Transformer`

The paper’s `Transformer-L` corresponds to FT-Transformer-style backbone.

### Embedding identifiers
The suffixes represent embedding families and optional extra differentiable layers:

- `L` = linear embedding
- `LR` = linear + ReLU
- `LRLR` = linear + ReLU + linear + ReLU
- `Q` = quantile PLE
- `T` = target-aware PLE
- `P` = periodic
- `PL` = periodic + linear
- `PLR` = periodic + linear + ReLU
- `Q-L`, `Q-LR`, `Q-LRLR`, etc.
- `T-L`, `T-LR`, `T-LRLR`, etc.

Important: the module should not hard-code an unrestricted parser that accepts arbitrary unknown forms. It should support the model names used in the paper and config-driven experiments.

---

### 6) Recommended composition rules

This module should enforce the paper’s composition semantics.

---

## 6.1 Baseline model without embedding enhancement
Examples:
- `MLP`
- `ResNet`
- `Transformer-L`

These should use the standard numerical feature handling from the chosen embedding/backbone implementation. In the context of this reproduction, if the embedding module still exists, it should be the simplest baseline embedding consistent with the backbone implementation.

---

## 6.2 Linear embedding family
Examples:
- `MLP-L`
- `MLP-LR`
- `MLP-LRLR`
- `ResNet-L`
- `ResNet-LR`
- `Transformer-LR`

These use the **LinearEmbedding** module as the base numerical embedding.

The extra suffix after the base embedding determines whether additional differentiable layers are stacked on top:
- `L` → plain linear embedding
- `LR` → linear embedding + ReLU
- `LRLR` → linear embedding + ReLU + linear + ReLU

The factory should construct these as embedding stacks, not as backbone modifications.

---

## 6.3 PLE family
Examples:
- `MLP-Q`
- `MLP-Q-LR`
- `MLP-Q-LRLR`
- `MLP-T`
- `MLP-T-LR`
- `MLP-T-LRLR`

The embedding base is `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding`.

The PLE variant requires one of two binning modes:
- quantile-based
- target-aware

The model name selects which one to use:
- `Q` → quantile bins
- `T` → target-aware bins

Then optional differentiable layers can be stacked after PLE, matching the paper’s naming.

For Transformer-like models, the factory must ensure the PLE output is compatible with token embeddings, which according to the paper means a projection layer after PLE.

---

## 6.4 Periodic family
Examples:
- `MLP-P`
- `MLP-PL`
- `MLP-PLR`
- `ResNet-P`
- `ResNet-PLR`
- `Transformer-PLR`

The base embedding is `PeriodicEmbedding`.

Then optional layers may be stacked:
- `P` → periodic only
- `PL` → periodic + linear
- `PLR` → periodic + linear + ReLU

The paper says `PL` can be viewed as a redundant linear layer that can be fused with the first backbone layer after training. Even so, the model should represent it explicitly in the architecture.

---

### 7) How the module should treat categorical features

The paper’s protocol says:

- CatBoost uses native categorical handling.
- All other algorithms use one-hot encoding.

For this module:
- It should accept `x_cat` in the `forward` signature because the shared design requires it.
- But the actual model may not need categorical input if preprocessing already one-hot encodes and concatenates features upstream.
- Therefore, the implementation should be consistent with the rest of the system and not assume categorical tensors are always used.

Practical consequence:
- `TabularModel.forward()` should be able to ignore `x_cat` if the preprocessor already merged everything into `x_num`-like tensors.
- If categorical tensors are present, the model should only process them if the broader design routes them into a compatible pathway. Since the provided design emphasizes numerical embeddings, the safest contract is to keep categorical handling minimal and external.

---

### 8) Backbone-specific routing logic

The factory must ensure the embedding output shape matches the backbone expectation.

---

## 8.1 MLP backbone
Expected behavior:
- embeddings for all numerical features are concatenated into one flat vector
- optional categorical features, if already encoded, should be concatenated upstream
- backbone consumes a 2D tensor `[batch, features]`

So for MLP:
- embedding module should yield flat concatenated representation
- backbone input dimension must match the resulting embedding dimension
- head is attached after backbone output

---

## 8.2 ResNet backbone
Expected behavior:
- same input shape convention as MLP
- typically consumes a flat vector, though internal residual blocks may transform it

So for ResNet:
- `TabularModel` should treat the embedding output the same as for MLP
- backbone will handle residual feature mixing internally

---

## 8.3 Transformer backbone
Expected behavior:
- consumes token sequence of feature embeddings
- embeddings should retain token structure `[batch, num_tokens, emb_dim]` or the equivalent representation used by the backbone implementation

For Transformer:
- the embedding module must produce per-feature token embeddings rather than a flattened vector
- for PLE-based variants, the paper requires a linear projection after PLE
- model factory must ensure the token dimension is the backbone’s embedding size from config-driven tuning

This is the main place where the model name must be interpreted carefully.

---

### 9) Output head logic

The model head must be task-aware.

---

## 9.1 Classification head
For classification tasks:
- output dimension = number of classes
- output should be raw logits
- loss is cross-entropy, handled by trainer

The module should not apply softmax in the model output because training uses cross-entropy directly.

---

## 9.2 Regression head
For regression tasks:
- output dimension = 1
- output should be a single scalar per sample
- no activation at the final layer

The evaluator and trainer will handle inverse target standardization if needed.

---

### 10) Forward-pass contract

`TabularModel.forward(x_num, x_cat=None)` should:

1. Send inputs through embedding module
2. Send embedded representation into backbone
3. Send backbone output into head
4. Return predictions

### Important constraints
- Do not mix training logic into `forward`
- Do not compute losses inside `forward`
- Do not apply output activations that would interfere with trainer-selected losses

---

### 11) Factory construction rules

The `ModelFactory` should derive the architecture as follows:

#### Step 1: Identify backbone
Parse the model name prefix:
- `MLP`
- `ResNet`
- `Transformer`

#### Step 2: Identify embedding family
Parse the suffix:
- none / baseline
- `L`, `LR`, `LRLR`
- `Q`, `Q-L`, `Q-LR`, `Q-LRLR`
- `T`, `T-L`, `T-LR`, `T-LRLR`
- `P`, `PL`, `PLR`

#### Step 3: Instantiate the correct embedding module
Based on:
- embedding family
- dataset-specific or tuning-time embedding parameters
- PLE bin construction mode
- periodic hyperparameters from config

#### Step 4: Instantiate the backbone
Based on:
- backbone type
- hyperparameters sampled/tuned in `training/tuning.py`
- dataset-specific config settings such as transformer layer/embedding-size ranges

#### Step 5: Determine head output size
Based on:
- `task_type` from config / dataset metadata

#### Step 6: Return `TabularModel`
The resulting model must be ready for trainer consumption.

---

### 12) Validation of model names

The module should fail early and clearly on unsupported names.

Recommended behavior:
- If the model name cannot be parsed, raise a descriptive error.
- If a suffix combination is invalid for the chosen backbone, raise a descriptive error.
- If required hyperparameters are missing from config, raise a descriptive error.

This is important because the experiment matrix is large and silent fallback would break reproducibility.

---

### 13) Interface expectations with other modules

This module must align with:

#### `models/embeddings.py`
Expected to provide:
- `BaseEmbedding`
- `LinearEmbedding`
- `PiecewiseLinearEmbedding`
- `PeriodicEmbedding`

The tabular model should not depend on implementation internals beyond their public constructors and forward methods.

#### `models/backbones.py`
Expected to provide:
- `MLPBackbone`
- `ResNetBackbone`
- `TransformerBackbone`

The backbone must accept the embedding output shape produced by the chosen embedding module.

#### `training/trainer.py`
The trainer expects the model to:
- be a standard `torch.nn.Module`
- accept `(x_num, x_cat=None)`
- return raw predictions suitable for the configured loss

---

### 14) Configuration-driven behavior from `config.yaml`

The following config sections are relevant to this module:

#### `models.mlp`
- `layers`
- `layer_size`
- `dropout`
- `weight_decay`

#### `models.resnet`
- `layers`
- `layer_size`
- `hidden_factor`
- `hidden_dropout`
- `residual_dropout`
- `weight_decay`

#### `models.transformer`
- `layers.sa_co_mi`
- `layers.other`
- `embedding_size.sa_co_mi`
- `embedding_size.other`
- `residual_dropout.sa_co_mi`
- `residual_dropout.other`
- `attention_dropout`
- `ffn_dropout`
- `ffn_factor`
- `weight_decay.sa_co_mi`
- `weight_decay.other`

#### `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces`
- `linear_embedding_output_dim`
- `ple_quantiles`
- `ple_tree.max_leaves`
- `ple_tree.min_items_per_leaf`
- `ple_tree.min_information_gain`
- `periodic_k`

This module should not tune these values itself, but it must accept them when passed from `ModelFactory`.

---

### 15) Practical implementation implications

Even though this is just the logic analysis, a few implementation-level facts matter:

1. **Keep `TabularModel` thin**
   - It should be a composition wrapper, not a second trainer or preprocessing layer.

2. **Keep `ModelFactory` deterministic**
   - Same config + same model name must always build the same component stack.

3. **Treat output head separately**
   - This avoids mixing representation learning with task-specific prediction logic.

4. **Do not assume every model uses the same embedding dimensionality**
   - Linear, PLE, and periodic modules may each produce different output shapes.

5. **Transformer variants need careful shape handling**
   - The embedding output should remain tokenized rather than flattened.

---

### 16) Recommended internal control flow

A correct call flow inside this module should look like:

1. `ModelFactory.create(config, data_metadata)`
2. Parse `config.model_name`
3. Select backbone class
4. Select embedding class
5. Instantiate embedding with dataset-aware and tuning-aware parameters
6. Instantiate backbone with config-driven dimensions
7. Instantiate prediction head based on task
8. Return `TabularModel(embedding, backbone, head)`

Then at runtime:

1. `Trainer` calls `model(x_num, x_cat)`
2. `TabularModel.forward()` calls embedding
3. Backbone processes embeddings
4. Head generates logits or regression outputs
5. Trainer computes loss

---

### 17) Summary of the key fidelity requirements

This file must preserve the following paper-specific behaviors:

- feature-wise independent numerical embeddings
- correct mapping from model names to architecture stacks
- PLE quantile vs target-aware distinction
- periodic embedding support with tunable parameters
- backbone-specific embedding output handling
- classification/regression output head correctness
- no leakage of preprocessing or training logic into the model wrapper

If you want, I can next turn this into a **concrete class-by-class implementation blueprint** for `TabularModel` and `ModelFactory`, still without writing code.

[ANALYSIS] training/trainer.py
============================================
## Logic Analysis: `training/trainer.py`

This module implements the full single-run training lifecycle for one tuned tabular model, following the paper’s protocol and the fixed configuration in `config.yaml`.

---

### 1) Purpose and scope

`Trainer` is responsible for:

- training one `TabularModel` instance on one dataset split
- using **AdamW**
- selecting the correct loss by task type
- validating after every epoch
- applying **early stopping with patience = 16**
- saving and restoring the **best validation checkpoint**
- producing final **test-set inference** after training

It does **not**:
- tune hyperparameters
- load or preprocess raw data
- create model architectures
- perform ensemble aggregation
- compute final metrics beyond collecting predictions / losses needed by `evaluation/metrics.py`

Those responsibilities belong to other modules in the design.

---

### 2) Configuration constraints to obey

All training logic must reference `config.yaml` and not invent values.

Relevant fixed settings:

- `training.optimizer = adamw`
- `training.loss.classification = cross_entropy`
- `training.loss.regression = mse`
- `training.early_stopping.patience = 16`
- `training.early_stopping.min_delta = 0.0`
- `training.lr_schedule = none`
- `training.max_epochs = 100`
- `evaluation.test_runs_per_tuned_config = 15`

Dataset-specific batch sizes must come from:

- `training.batch_size.<dataset>`

Learning rate and weight decay are model-family dependent and are produced by tuning/model configuration, not by this trainer itself.

---

### 3) Required inputs and expected data contract

The trainer must operate on the preprocessed data structure from `data/preprocessing.py`:

- numerical tensors
- categorical tensors if present
- targets for train / val / test
- metadata sufficient to infer task behavior

The trainer should assume the preprocessing stage has already handled:

- quantile transform / no preprocessing for Otto
- one-hot encoding for categorical features for non-CatBoost models
- target standardization for regression

Therefore, the trainer should **not** alter data preprocessing.

---

### 4) Model interface assumptions

The trainer will consume the `TabularModel` interface from `models/tabular_model.py`.

It should rely only on the interface defined in the design:

- forward pass accepts numerical and optional categorical tensors
- outputs logits for classification and predictions for regression

The trainer must not assume any extra public methods beyond those in the design.  
Training should only rely on calling the model directly through `forward(...)`.

---

### 5) Loss selection logic

The trainer must choose loss strictly by `task_type`:

#### Classification
Use:
- `torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss`

Expected output:
- model outputs raw logits of shape `[batch_size, num_classes]`
- targets are class indices of shape `[batch_size]`

#### Regression
Use:
- `torch.nn.MSELoss`

Expected output:
- model outputs scalar predictions of shape `[batch_size]` or `[batch_size, 1]`
- targets are float tensors with matching shape

#### Microsoft ranking dataset
The paper says Microsoft is treated as regression.  
So the trainer should still use the regression branch for `task_type="regression"`.

---

### 6) Optimizer logic

The only optimizer to use is:

- `torch.optim.AdamW`

This must match the paper and config.

Optimizer parameters:

- model parameters
- learning rate from the tuned model config
- weight decay from the tuned model config

No learning-rate scheduler is used, because:

- `training.lr_schedule = none`

So the trainer must not instantiate or step any scheduler.

---

### 7) Batch size handling

Batch size is dataset-specific and must be read from:

- `training.batch_size`

The trainer should not hardcode batch sizes.

It should assume batches are already assembled through a dataloader or batch iterator coming from the surrounding pipeline, or internally create one if that is part of the implementation plan. Either way, the dataset key must map to the configured batch size.

The paper’s protocol uses the same batch size per dataset for all deep models.

---

### 8) Training loop behavior

The `train()` method should implement:

1. initialize optimizer and loss
2. move model and tensors to device
3. run epochs up to `training.max_epochs = 100`
4. each epoch:
   - call `fit_one_epoch()`
   - call `validate()`
   - compare validation score to current best
   - save checkpoint if improved
   - update early stopping counter if not improved
5. stop when patience is exceeded
6. restore the best checkpoint
7. evaluate on the test set

This matches the paper’s use of validation-based early stopping and final test reporting.

---

### 9) Validation and early stopping criteria

The paper states:

- stop after `patience + 1` consecutive epochs without validation improvement
- patience is fixed to `16`

Config additionally gives:

- `min_delta = 0.0`

Therefore:

- a validation improvement is any strictly better score by more than `0.0`
- the score to monitor is the validation metric appropriate for the task

The trainer should use the **validation objective direction** consistent with the metric:

- Accuracy: higher is better
- RMSE: lower is better

Because `validate()` is part of training and not final metrics reporting, it should return a scalar that the trainer can compare consistently.

Recommended internal logic:
- for classification: maximize validation accuracy or equivalent validation score
- for regression: minimize validation RMSE

The trainer should not infer alternative metrics not present in config.

---

### 10) `fit_one_epoch()` logic

This method should handle one full pass over the training data.

Responsibilities:

- set model to train mode
- iterate over training batches
- move batch tensors to the selected device
- forward pass
- compute task-appropriate loss
- backward pass
- optimizer step
- accumulate training loss statistics

It should return at least a summary loss value, possibly averaged over batches.

Important:
- do not evaluate on validation/test inside this method
- do not perform checkpointing here
- do not modify seeds here

---

### 11) `validate()` logic

This method should:

- switch the model to eval mode
- disable gradient computation
- run over the validation split
- collect predictions and targets
- compute validation loss and/or validation metric

Its output must be compatible with early stopping.

For classification:
- likely compute average cross-entropy or accuracy
- but whichever scalar is used for model selection must be consistent

For regression:
- likely compute RMSE on the validation split

The paper uses validation for tuning and early stopping, so this function should be deterministic for a given seed and fixed model state.

---

### 12) `save_checkpoint()` and `load_best_checkpoint()`

These are part of the required public API and must work as follows:

#### `save_checkpoint(path)`
Save the best model state and enough metadata to recover training state if needed.

At minimum, checkpoint should include:
- model state dict
- optimizer state dict if needed for resuming
- best validation score
- current epoch
- any task-specific metadata necessary for restoration

#### `load_best_checkpoint(path)`
Restore the best saved weights into the current model.

This should be used after training is complete, before test inference.

Because the paper’s final evaluation uses the best validation configuration, not the last epoch, checkpoint restoration is mandatory.

---

### 13) Final test inference

After the best checkpoint is restored, the trainer should produce predictions on the test split.

Important:
- test data must not influence training or checkpoint selection
- test evaluation happens once per trained seed after early stopping selects the best model
- final metric computation itself belongs to `evaluation/metrics.py`, but trainer should provide the predictions needed

For the 15-seed protocol:
- the outer orchestration in `main.py` will call the trainer multiple times with different seeds
- trainer should remain single-run and seed-agnostic beyond receiving the seed from config

---

### 14) Reproducibility requirements

The trainer depends on `utils/reproducibility.py`, so it must assume:

- random seed is already set before training begins
- deterministic behavior has been requested if applicable
- device selection is already resolved

The trainer itself should avoid introducing extra nondeterminism:
- keep dataloader shuffling controlled by the seed
- avoid stochastic operations outside model-defined dropout behavior
- do not use random sampling inside training logic

---

### 15) Data flow inside the trainer

The typical flow is:

1. receive model and preprocessed data
2. build loss and optimizer from config
3. loop over epochs:
   - train one epoch
   - validate
   - compare against best
   - checkpoint if improved
4. restore best weights
5. run test inference
6. return training history / predictions / best score

This aligns with the paper’s “validation for tuning, test for final evaluation” protocol.

---

### 16) Output artifacts expected from Trainer

The trainer should support returning or storing:

- best validation score
- epoch of best validation
- final test predictions
- history of train/validation loss or metric per epoch
- checkpoint path used

These are useful for:
- Optuna tuning in `training/tuning.py`
- final result aggregation in `main.py`
- ensemble construction in `evaluation/ensemble.py`

---

### 17) Edge cases to handle

The trainer must be robust to:

- regression targets with shape issues after standardization
- classification target dtype requirements for cross-entropy
- binary classification being treated as classification, not regression
- test split existing but not used until after training
- datasets with categorical features already one-hot encoded by preprocessing
- no validation improvement for the full patience window
- best checkpoint occurring at epoch 0 or early in training

---

### 18) Non-goals for this file

Do not implement here:

- dataset downloading
- preprocessing transforms
- Optuna search space sampling
- ensemble averaging
- CatBoost/XGBoost training
- metric table formatting
- model architecture details beyond invoking the supplied model

Those are separate modules by design.

---

### 19) Summary of the implementation contract

`training/trainer.py` must provide a single-run, config-driven training engine that:

- uses `AdamW`
- uses cross-entropy for classification and MSE for regression
- trains for up to 100 epochs
- validates every epoch
- early-stops with patience 16
- saves/restores the best validation model
- returns final test inference outputs for downstream evaluation
- respects the paper’s no-scheduler, seed-controlled, reproducible protocol

This module is central to reproducing the paper because all tuned models, repeated seeds, and final comparisons depend on it.
============================================

# Logic Analysis: `training/tuning.py`

This module is responsible for reproducing the paper’s **hyperparameter tuning protocol** using **Optuna TPE** and the paper’s dataset/model-specific search spaces. It must produce a best configuration per dataset/model variant by optimizing **validation performance only**, then pass that best configuration to final training and the repeated-seed evaluation pipeline.

---

## 1) Role of this module in the reproduction pipeline

`training/tuning.py` sits between:

1. **Data preprocessing** (`data/preprocessing.py`)
2. **Model construction** (`models/tabular_model.py`)
3. **Training/evaluation** (`training/trainer.py`, `evaluation/metrics.py`)

Its responsibilities are:

- define the Optuna objective
- sample hyperparameters according to the paper/config
- train one candidate model per trial
- evaluate on validation set only
- return the best hyperparameters/configuration
- preserve exact dataset-dependent search spaces and model-family-specific choices

This module must not:
- use test data for tuning
- modify the paper’s training protocol
- invent any additional search dimensions beyond those in `config.yaml` and the paper text

---

## 2) Required inputs and outputs

### Inputs
The tuner should operate using:

- the shared experiment config schema
- dataset identity (`dataset_name`)
- task type (`classification`, `regression`)
- model name (`model_name`)
- preprocessed train/validation data
- model family information:
  - `mlp`
  - `resnet`
  - `transformer`
- embedding type / model variant naming:
  - e.g. `MLP-LR`, `MLP-Q-LR`, `Transformer-T-LR`, etc.

### Outputs
For each tuned experiment, the tuner should return:

- best hyperparameter configuration
- best validation score
- optionally best trial metadata for logging/saving

The returned config must be compatible with `ModelFactory` and `Trainer`.

---

## 3) Strict configuration sources to use

This module must reference values only from `config.yaml`:

### Experiment control
- `experiment.seed = 42`
- `experiment.num_seeds = 15`
- `experiment.num_ensembles = 3`
- `experiment.ensemble_group_size = 5`

### Tuning control
- `hyperparameter_tuning.method = optuna_tpe`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.trials = 100`

### Search spaces
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.linear_embedding_output_dim = [1, 128]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_quantiles = [2, 256]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.max_leaves = [2, 256]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.min_items_per_leaf = [1, 128]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.ple_tree.min_information_gain = [1.0e-09, 0.01]`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.search_spaces.periodic_k = [1, 128]`

### Model-specific ranges
- `models.mlp.layers = [1, 16]`
- `models.mlp.layer_size = [1, 1024]`
- `models.mlp.dropout = [0.0, 0.5]`
- `models.mlp.weight_decay = [0.0, 1.0e-03]`

- `models.resnet.layers = [1, 8]`
- `models.resnet.layer_size = [32, 512]`
- `models.resnet.hidden_factor = [1.0, 4.0]`
- `models.resnet.hidden_dropout = [0.0, 0.5]`
- `models.resnet.residual_dropout = [0.0, 0.5]`
- `models.resnet.weight_decay = [0.0, 1.0e-03]`

- `models.transformer.layers.sa_co_mi = [2, 4]`
- `models.transformer.layers.other = [1, 4]`
- `models.transformer.embedding_size.sa_co_mi = [192, 512]`
- `models.transformer.embedding_size.other = [96, 512]`
- `models.transformer.residual_dropout.sa_co_mi = 0.0`
- `models.transformer.residual_dropout.other = [0.0, 0.2]`
- `models.transformer.attention_dropout = [0.0, 0.5]`
- `models.transformer.ffn_dropout = [0.0, 0.5]`
- `models.transformer.ffn_factor = [0.6666667, 2.6666667]`
- `models.transformer.weight_decay.sa_co_mi = 1.0e-05`
- `models.transformer.weight_decay.other = [1.0e-06, 1.0e-04]`

### Optimization / early stopping
- optimizer: `adamw`
- no LR schedule
- `training.early_stopping.patience = 16`
- `training.early_stopping.min_delta = 0.0`
- `training.max_epochs = 100`

### Validation selection
- `evaluation.validation_selection = best_validation_score`

---

## 4) Core tuning logic

The paper uses **Optuna TPE** and selects hyperparameters by validation score. Therefore the tuner should:

1. create an Optuna study
2. define an objective function
3. sample a trial configuration
4. build the corresponding model
5. train with early stopping
6. evaluate validation metric
7. return a scalar objective value consistent with the metric direction

### Direction of optimization
The module must optimize according to the metric:

- **classification**: maximize accuracy
- **regression**: minimize RMSE

This implies the objective must map scores correctly:
- for accuracy, higher is better
- for RMSE, lower is better

The tuner should align with `MetricsEvaluator` for score computation.

---

## 5) Dataset-specific search space branching

The paper and config specify different tuning ranges for some datasets, especially Transformer.

### Datasets in the special Transformer group
The config uses the special group:
- `sa`
- `co`
- `mi`

For these datasets:
- smaller LR range
- larger embedding size range
- fixed residual dropout = 0.0
- fixed weight decay = 1e-5

For all other datasets:
- wider LR range
- different embedding size range
- residual dropout sampled from `[0.0, 0.2]`
- weight decay sampled from `[1e-6, 1e-4]`

The tuner must branch on `dataset_name` exactly using these rules.

---

## 6) Hyperparameters to sample by model family

This module must handle tuning depending on both:

- the **backbone**
- the **embedding module**
- the **dataset group**

### 6.1 MLP family
For MLP-based models, tune from config:

- number of layers
- layer size
- dropout
- weight decay
- learning rate

If the variant includes linear embedding or linear layers on top of embeddings, also tune:
- `linear_embedding_output_dim`

For the embedding-specific variants:
- **PLE-q**: tune `ple_quantiles`
- **PLE-t**: tune `ple_tree.max_leaves`, `ple_tree.min_items_per_leaf`, `ple_tree.min_information_gain`
- **Periodic**: tune `periodic_k`
- also the periodic module needs its paper-specific sigma parameter if it exists in the implementation; however, since `config.yaml` does not provide a search space for sigma, this module must either:
  - use a fixed implementation default, or
  - defer to a model-level default already encoded elsewhere
  but it must not invent a sigma range in this file

The paper says periodic has `sigma` and `k`, but only `k` is explicitly present in the config, so this is a constraint to respect.

### 6.2 ResNet family
Tune:

- number of layers
- layer size
- hidden factor
- hidden dropout
- residual dropout
- weight decay
- learning rate

And the same embedding-specific hyperparameters as above when applicable:
- linear embedding output dim
- PLE quantiles / tree bins
- periodic k

### 6.3 Transformer family
Tune:

- number of layers
- embedding size
- residual dropout
- attention dropout
- FFN dropout
- FFN factor
- weight decay
- learning rate

And embedding-specific hyperparameters when applicable:
- linear embedding output dim
- PLE quantiles / tree bins
- periodic k

---

## 7) Model-name parsing responsibility

The tuner needs a deterministic way to interpret the model name.

Examples from the paper:
- `MLP`
- `MLP-LR`
- `MLP-Q-LR`
- `MLP-T-LR`
- `MLP-P`
- `MLP-PLR`
- `ResNet-PLR`
- `Transformer-T-LR`

The tuner must not implement model logic directly. Instead, it should:

1. read the `model_name`
2. determine backbone family
3. determine embedding family
4. sample hyperparameters relevant to that family only

This should be consistent with `ModelFactory`, but the tuner needs enough awareness to know what to sample.

---

## 8) Objective function structure

The objective function should follow this flow:

1. receive an Optuna trial
2. sample all relevant hyperparameters
3. merge sampled values into a deep copy of the base config
4. build the model with `ModelFactory`
5. instantiate `Trainer`
6. train until early stopping or max epochs
7. compute validation score using `MetricsEvaluator`
8. return the score in the correct optimization direction

### Important
The objective must use:
- training split for fitting
- validation split for scoring
- no test access

### Returned scalar
The objective should be a single scalar:
- accuracy directly for classification
- RMSE directly for regression if minimizing
  - if Optuna study is configured as `minimize`, return RMSE
  - if configured as `maximize`, return negative RMSE
The design should choose one consistent convention. The safest is:
- use Optuna study direction matching the metric:
  - `maximize` for accuracy
  - `minimize` for RMSE

This avoids negation mistakes.

---

## 9) Handling repeated runs and final evaluation

This tuner is only for selecting hyperparameters. After tuning:

- the best config is reused for **15 seeds**
- final test results are computed elsewhere
- ensemble logic is handled elsewhere

Therefore `training/tuning.py` should expose results sufficient for downstream execution:
- best_params
- best_trial
- best_validation_score

It should not aggregate over 15 seeds itself.

---

## 10) Required interactions with other modules

### With `Trainer`
The tuner depends on trainer to:
- run a full candidate training
- stop early based on validation

The trainer must already support:
- train
- validate
- checkpointing best validation weights

The tuner should treat `Trainer` as the execution engine and not duplicate its logic.

### With `ModelFactory`
The tuner should not manually instantiate embeddings/backbones. It should:
- pass a config containing sampled hyperparameters
- let `ModelFactory` construct the correct composed model

### With `MetricsEvaluator`
The tuner should rely on a single metric computation pathway so that:
- tuning validation score
- final test score
- ensemble score
all use consistent definitions.

---

## 11) Copying and mutation rules

Because each trial modifies configuration values, the tuner must:

- deep copy the base configuration for every trial
- never mutate the global config in-place
- isolate trial-specific values from other trials

This is important because:
- multiple trials run sequentially in one study
- Optuna objective state must remain independent

---

## 12) Suggested internal data flow

For each objective call:

1. `trial -> sampled hyperparameters`
2. `base_config deep copied`
3. `trial params inserted into model/training config`
4. `ModelFactory.create(config, data)` returns model
5. `Trainer.train(data)` returns validation history / best checkpoint
6. `MetricsEvaluator` computes final validation score
7. objective returns score

The tuner should also persist the trial summary if logging is enabled through the broader pipeline.

---

## 13) Search-space implementation details

### Integer parameters
Use Optuna integer suggestions for:
- layers
- layer size
- periodic_k
- PLE quantiles
- PLE tree leaves
- PLE tree min items per leaf
- PLE tree min info gain?  
  This one is continuous, so use a float suggestion on a log scale if implementing via Optuna.

### Float parameters
Use Optuna float suggestions for:
- dropout
- hidden_factor
- residual_dropout
- attention_dropout
- ffn_dropout
- ffn_factor
- weight decay
- learning rate
- min information gain

### Conditional parameters
Some parameters are conditional on model family or dataset group:

- Transformer layers and embedding size vary by dataset group
- Transformer residual dropout and weight decay vary by dataset group
- embedding-specific search dimensions only apply when the model includes those embeddings

---

## 14) Paper fidelity constraints

The tuner must reflect the paper’s tuning protocol:

- TPE sampler
- 100 trials
- validation-based selection
- no test leakage
- model-specific search spaces
- dataset-specific transformer search space
- 15-seed final evaluation after tuning
- early stopping patience = 16
- max epochs = 100
- no LR schedule

These are not optional.

---

## 15) Edge cases to handle

### Regression vs classification
The tuner must be able to switch objective direction based on task type.

### Otto preprocessing exception
Since Otto has no feature preprocessing, tuning must still proceed with the corresponding preprocessed data object.

### Microsoft dataset
Treat as regression per the paper; tuning should use RMSE.

### Datasets with categorical features
Tuning should not change preprocessing behavior; it only tunes model parameters.

### Variant names without extra embedding parameters
For plain backbones like `MLP`, `ResNet`, `Transformer-L`, the tuner should avoid injecting unnecessary embedding-search params.

---

## 16) Output artifacts recommended

Although not required in the interface, the tuner should be designed to produce:

- best hyperparameters dict
- best validation score
- Optuna study object or summary
- optionally per-trial logs

These are useful for:
- reproducibility
- later table generation
- rerunning final evaluations with the selected config

---

## 17) Non-goals for this file

This file should not:
- implement dataset downloading
- implement preprocessing transformations
- implement embedding math
- implement the full training loop
- implement ensemble evaluation
- implement GBDT baselines

Those belong in their designated modules.

---

## 18) Final implementation takeaway

`training/tuning.py` should be a **thin orchestration layer** over Optuna, `ModelFactory`, and `Trainer`, with careful conditional sampling based on:

- dataset group
- backbone family
- embedding variant

Its central requirement is to reproduce the paper’s hyperparameter tuning faithfully, using only the values explicitly given in `config.yaml`, and to ensure that the final chosen configuration is valid for downstream repeated-seed evaluation.
============================================
