[ANALYSIS] evaluation/metrics.py
============================================
### Logic Analysis: `evaluation/metrics.py`

This module is responsible for all metric computations used during:

- validation-based hyperparameter tuning
- final test evaluation across 15 seeds
- ensemble evaluation
- summary reporting across seeds

It must stay strictly aligned with the paper and `config.yaml`.

---

## 1) Purpose and scope

`MetricsEvaluator` should provide a single, consistent metric interface for all experiments so that:

- **classification** datasets are evaluated with **accuracy**
- **regression** datasets are evaluated with **RMSE**
- the **Microsoft MSLR-WEB10K** task is treated as regression, as stated in the paper
- seed-level results can be aggregated into mean / standard deviation summaries

This module should not contain any model logic, preprocessing logic, or dataset loading logic. It only evaluates predictions against labels.

---

## 2) Configuration-driven behavior

Use only the evaluation-related settings explicitly present in `config.yaml`:

- `evaluation.metrics.classification = accuracy`
- `evaluation.metrics.regression = rmse`
- `evaluation.validation_selection = best_validation_score`
- `evaluation.test_runs_per_tuned_config = 15`

The paper and config imply:

- validation metric is the same metric used for selecting the best model
- for classification, “best” means higher accuracy
- for regression, “best” means lower RMSE
- the evaluation module should expose a clear rule for whether higher or lower is better

Do not invent additional metrics or thresholds.

---

## 3) Required public interface

Per design, this file must define `MetricsEvaluator` with these methods:

### `compute_classification(y_true, y_pred) -> dict`
Computes classification metric(s).

Expected behavior:

- use **accuracy**
- accept predicted labels or class logits/probabilities depending on how the trainer calls it
- if probabilities/logits are passed, the caller should already convert them to class labels where appropriate, unless the implementation explicitly supports conversion
- return a dictionary so that future extension is possible, but the required primary output should be accuracy

Recommended output structure:
- `{"accuracy": float}`

### `compute_regression(y_true, y_pred) -> dict`
Computes regression metric(s).

Expected behavior:

- use **RMSE**
- support both scalar regression targets and Microsoft’s ranking-as-regression setting
- return a dictionary with the primary metric

Recommended output structure:
- `{"rmse": float}`

### `select_metric(task_type) -> str`
Returns the metric name that should be used for model selection.

Expected mapping:
- classification → `accuracy`
- regression → `rmse`

This is important because training/tuning code should not have to branch on metric names in multiple places.

---

## 4) Metric definitions

### 4.1 Classification: Accuracy

For classification datasets:

- metric = fraction of exact matches between predicted labels and ground truth labels
- use `sklearn.metrics.accuracy_score`

This aligns with the paper’s tables for datasets such as GE, CH, AD, OT, HI, FB, SA.

Important implementation detail:

- the metric should be computed on the **final predicted class labels**
- if model outputs logits/probabilities, conversion to labels should happen consistently in the caller or via a small helper if the interface supports it

### 4.2 Regression: RMSE

For regression datasets:

- metric = root mean squared error
- use `sklearn.metrics.mean_squared_error(..., squared=False)` or equivalent
- return the positive root value

This applies to:
- CA
- HO
- MI

### 4.3 Microsoft ranking-as-regression

The paper explicitly says Microsoft is treated as regression using the pointwise approach.

Therefore:

- no ranking metric should be introduced here
- compute RMSE exactly like other regression datasets
- ensure the caller does not attempt to interpret it as a ranking metric

---

## 5) Summary statistics over seeds

The paper reports results over **15 random seeds** per tuned configuration, and often uses mean and standard deviation.

This module should therefore support summarizing a list of per-seed metric values.

Even though the design only explicitly requires `compute_classification`, `compute_regression`, and `select_metric`, the logic should include a clean internal convention for seed summaries, because the training pipeline and final tables require it.

Expected summary behavior:

- input: list/array of scalar metric values across seeds
- output: mean and standard deviation
- for classification: mean accuracy and std
- for regression: mean RMSE and std

Recommended summary form:
- `{"mean": float, "std": float}`

Be careful that:
- for accuracy, higher is better
- for RMSE, lower is better
- summary statistics should not flip the sign or convert RMSE to negative values unless the training loop explicitly requires a maximization objective

---

## 6) Model selection logic compatibility

The paper and config use `best_validation_score`, but the metric direction differs by task:

- classification: maximize accuracy
- regression: minimize RMSE

`MetricsEvaluator` should therefore support a consistent interpretation of “best” by exposing the metric name, while the trainer/tuner handles the direction correctly.

Suggested logic:
- `select_metric("classification") -> "accuracy"`
- `select_metric("regression") -> "rmse"`

Then the trainer/tuner should know:
- accuracy is better when larger
- rmse is better when smaller

This module should not hardcode tuning logic, but it should clearly return the correct metric label.

---

## 7) Input expectations

### `y_true`
- NumPy array-like
- shape `[n_samples]`
- classification labels or regression targets
- should be comparable directly with predictions

### `y_pred`
- for classification: label predictions, or possibly probability/logit outputs if the higher-level pipeline passes them in
- for regression: float predictions
- shape `[n_samples]`

The module should assume predictions have already been formatted appropriately by the caller whenever possible to keep responsibilities clean.

---

## 8) Edge cases and robustness

The evaluator should be resilient to common issues:

### Classification
- If predictions are one-hot vectors or probabilities, either:
  - the caller converts them to labels, or
  - the evaluator provides a clear, deterministic conversion rule (`argmax`)
- Ensure labels and predictions are flattened before scoring

### Regression
- Ensure both arrays are cast to floating point
- Flatten to 1D before RMSE computation

### Empty inputs
- Should not occur in normal training/evaluation
- If encountered, raise a clear error rather than silently returning invalid metrics

### NaNs / infs
- These should be treated as upstream errors
- The evaluator should not try to impute or sanitize them silently

---

## 9) Per-seed reporting behavior

The evaluation pipeline will likely call this module once per seed and then aggregate externally.

So this module should focus on:
- per-run metric computation
- optional helper for aggregating multiple runs

Do not mix:
- tuning-time validation metric computation
- test-time metric computation
- ensemble metric computation

They should all use the same metric definitions, but remain separable in the control flow.

---

## 10) Compatibility with ensemble evaluation

The ensemble evaluator will average predictions across models and then call this module.

Therefore this module must remain agnostic to whether predictions come from:
- one model
- an ensemble of 5 models
- averaged predictions across 3 ensembles

For classification ensembles:
- final predictions should be label predictions after averaging probabilities or logits as handled upstream

For regression ensembles:
- final predictions are averaged numeric outputs

Then:
- classification → accuracy
- regression → RMSE

---

## 11) Recommended internal helper logic

Even though only three public methods are required, the module may benefit from internal helpers such as:

- `_prepare_arrays(y_true, y_pred)`
- `_to_class_labels(y_pred)`
- `_rmse(y_true, y_pred)`

These should stay private/internal and not expand the public API beyond the design.

---

## 12) Alignment with paper and config

This module must enforce the following paper/config constraints:

- Use **accuracy** for all classification datasets
- Use **RMSE** for all regression datasets
- Treat **Microsoft** as regression
- Compute metrics consistently across:
  - tuning
  - 15-seed final evaluation
  - ensemble evaluation
- Do not introduce additional metrics, averaging schemes, or task-specific exceptions

---

## 13) Final implementation intent

`evaluation/metrics.py` should become the single canonical metric source for the entire reproduction pipeline. Its job is simple but critical:

1. map task type to the right metric
2. compute that metric correctly
3. provide seed-level summary statistics in a consistent format

This ensures that the tuning, training, and reporting stages all evaluate models in the same way, which is essential for faithful reproduction of the paper’s experiments.
============================================

[ANALYSIS] evaluation/ensemble.py
============================================
### Logic Analysis for `evaluation/ensemble.py`

This module must implement the **3-way ensemble evaluation protocol** described in the paper and constrained by `config.yaml`.

---

## 1) Purpose of this module

`evaluation/ensemble.py` is responsible for:

1. Taking predictions from **15 trained seeds** for a tuned model-dataset pair.
2. Splitting those 15 runs into **3 disjoint groups of 5**.
3. Averaging predictions within each group to form **3 ensemble predictions**.
4. Evaluating each ensemble using the correct metric for the task:
   - **Accuracy** for classification
   - **RMSE** for regression
5. Returning ensemble metrics in a format compatible with the rest of the evaluation pipeline.

This module must **not** perform training, tuning, or preprocessing. It only aggregates already-computed predictions and evaluates them.

---

## 2) Paper-aligned behavior

The paper explicitly states:

- “For each model-dataset pair, we obtain three ensembles by splitting the 15 single models into three disjoint groups of equal size and averaging predictions of single models within each group.”

The logic must therefore follow these exact constraints:

- Exactly **15 predictions** per model-dataset pair are expected for ensemble construction.
- The 15 predictions are partitioned into **3 groups of 5**.
- No overlapping between groups.
- Ensemble score is computed on the averaged predictions of each group.

The `config.yaml` confirms this with:

- `experiment.num_seeds: 15`
- `experiment.num_ensembles: 3`
- `experiment.ensemble_group_size: 5`

So the implementation must use these values as the expected protocol, not hardcoded arbitrary values.

---

## 3) Input/output expectations

### Expected input
The module should accept prediction arrays from multiple seeds, likely in a structure such as:

- a list of 15 arrays
- each array corresponding to one seed’s test predictions
- each array having the same shape and alignment with the test targets

Because the design spec does not define extra public interfaces, the ensemble logic should be implemented within the allowed class methods of `EnsembleEvaluator`.

### Expected output
The evaluator should return:

- per-ensemble metrics for the 3 groups
- optionally an aggregate summary across the 3 ensembles

The exact return format should align with the rest of the project’s metric reporting, but the key requirement is that it must support:
- metric computation for each ensemble
- group-wise averaging result
- final metric dictionary for downstream logging/saving

---

## 4) Core ensemble logic

### 4.1 Grouping logic
Given 15 seed-level prediction vectors:

- group 1 = predictions from seeds 1–5
- group 2 = predictions from seeds 6–10
- group 3 = predictions from seeds 11–15

The paper only says “split into three disjoint groups of equal size” and does not specify random shuffling at this step. Therefore:

- if upstream code preserves seed order, this module should **respect the input order**
- if the project wants deterministic grouping, it should group by the order in which predictions are supplied
- do **not** introduce random regrouping here unless explicitly required elsewhere

This keeps the behavior reproducible and consistent with the paper.

---

### 4.2 Averaging predictions
For each group:

- stack the 5 prediction arrays
- compute the arithmetic mean along the seed axis

This averaged prediction is the ensemble prediction for that group.

#### For classification:
The module must preserve compatibility with the metric implementation in `evaluation/metrics.py`.

Two possibilities exist upstream:

1. The model returns probabilities/logits and the evaluator converts to class labels for accuracy.
2. The model already returns class labels.

Because the design and paper emphasize consistent metric handling, the ensemble evaluator should **delegate final metric interpretation to `MetricsEvaluator`** rather than hardcoding label thresholding here, except where necessary and already consistent with the prediction format used elsewhere.

For binary classification, if predictions are probabilistic, the same thresholding policy used in the rest of the pipeline must be applied consistently.

#### For regression:
- The ensemble prediction is the mean of the 5 scalar predictions
- Metric is RMSE against ground truth

---

## 5) Metric computation responsibilities

This module must depend on `evaluation.metrics` and use it as the single source of truth for metric computation.

### Required behavior:
- Determine task type:
  - classification → accuracy
  - regression → RMSE
- Call the appropriate function in `MetricsEvaluator`
- Avoid duplicating metric formulas here

This is important for consistency because the paper compares:
- single models
- tuned models
- ensembles

All of these should be evaluated with the exact same metric logic.

---

## 6) Configuration-driven constraints

The module must align with the values in `config.yaml`:

- `experiment.num_seeds = 15`
- `experiment.num_ensembles = 3`
- `experiment.ensemble_group_size = 5`

The ensemble evaluator should validate these assumptions logically:

- number of prediction sets should match `num_seeds`
- number of ensembles should match `num_ensembles`
- each group should contain `ensemble_group_size` predictions

If the provided predictions do not match this expected layout, the module should fail loudly or raise a clear error, because the paper’s ensemble protocol is fixed.

---

## 7) Suggested internal flow

The internal logic should be:

1. Receive predictions and targets.
2. Verify the count of prediction arrays is 15.
3. Partition predictions into 3 groups of 5.
4. Average predictions within each group.
5. For each group:
   - compute the metric using `MetricsEvaluator`
6. Return:
   - per-group metrics
   - optionally their mean/std if needed by the reporting layer

This module should stay lightweight and deterministic.

---

## 8) Shape and type considerations

To avoid evaluation bugs, the evaluator must ensure:

- all prediction arrays have identical shapes
- all predictions correspond to the same test sample order
- regression outputs are numeric vectors
- classification outputs are in the format expected by `MetricsEvaluator`

If predictions are torch tensors upstream, they should already be converted to numpy arrays before entering this module, or converted here only if the design allows it. Given the design list and shared architecture, this module should preferably operate on `numpy.ndarray` inputs.

---

## 9) What this module should not do

To remain faithful to the design:

- It should **not** train models
- It should **not** tune hyperparameters
- It should **not** perform data splitting
- It should **not** implement preprocessing
- It should **not** alter the seed-level predictions beyond averaging within groups
- It should **not** invent a different ensemble scheme such as:
  - majority vote across all 15
  - stacking
  - weighted averaging
  - cross-validated blending

The paper uses a simple equal-weight mean ensemble only.

---

## 10) Reproducibility note

The ensemble procedure is deterministic **given the prediction list order**.

Therefore:
- reproducibility depends on upstream seed ordering being fixed
- the evaluator should not shuffle or sample internally

This matches the paper’s deterministic reporting style and ensures the ensemble results are traceable.

---

## 11) Expected integration points

This module will be used after training and inference by `main.py`:

1. train 15 seeds
2. collect test predictions
3. pass them to `EnsembleEvaluator`
4. obtain ensemble metrics
5. log results alongside single-model metrics and baselines

It should also work in the DL-vs-GBDT comparison setting where ensemble performance is reported separately from single-model performance.

---

## 12) Final implementation intent

`evaluation/ensemble.py` should implement a clean, deterministic, paper-faithful ensemble evaluator that:

- assumes exactly 15 seed-level predictions
- creates 3 ensembles of 5 models each
- averages predictions within each ensemble
- computes accuracy or RMSE through `evaluation.metrics`
- returns a structured summary suitable for the experiment logger

This keeps the module narrowly focused and fully aligned with the paper and `config.yaml`.

[ANALYSIS] baselines/gbdt.py
============================================
## Logic Analysis: `baselines/gbdt.py`

### 1) Module responsibility
This file implements the GBDT comparison baselines used in the paper:
- **CatBoost**
- **XGBoost**

Its job is to:
1. Train the baseline model on the same dataset splits used by deep models.
2. Respect the paper’s preprocessing and target handling rules.
3. Tune hyperparameters using the **same validation-driven protocol**.
4. Return validation/test metrics in a format consistent with the rest of the system.
5. Support dataset-specific behavior:
   - **CatBoost** categorical-feature support
   - **XGBoost** GPU tuning on **MI** and **CO**
   - fixed early stopping / iteration settings from `config.yaml`

This module should not introduce any new experimental logic beyond what is specified in the paper and config.

---

### 2) Required inputs and shared assumptions
`GBDDBaselineRunner` must operate on the same split and preprocessing outputs as the deep models.

It should assume:
- The dataset has already been split into train / validation / test.
- Preprocessing has already been applied consistently according to dataset rules.
- Regression targets have been standardized if `config.data.target.regression_standardization = true`.
- Categorical columns are available in a form that CatBoost can consume, while non-CatBoost paths use one-hot encoded inputs if that is how preprocessing is configured.

Important alignment points from the config:
- `notes.implementation_constraints`: same preprocessing for all deep models within a dataset
- `otto_group_product_classification` has **no preprocessing**
- `evaluation.test_runs_per_tuned_config = 15` applies to final reported tuned configurations
- Baselines should still follow validation selection, early stopping, and tuning protocol

---

### 3) Public interface to implement
Per the design, this module must provide:

- `run_catboost()`
- `run_xgboost()`

No additional public methods should be assumed beyond the design.

Both functions should:
- accept preprocessed data and experiment configuration
- perform tuning using the validation split
- train the best model
- evaluate on test
- return metrics and best hyperparameters

If the runner class is used as a wrapper, its API should still expose only these two public entry methods.

---

### 4) Data flow for CatBoost
CatBoost is the simpler baseline because it can directly use categorical features.

#### 4.1 Training data handling
CatBoost should receive:
- numerical features
- categorical features in raw or integer-encoded form, depending on the preprocessing pipeline
- labels for train/val/test

For CatBoost:
- **do not one-hot encode categorical features**
- use CatBoost’s built-in categorical support
- numeric preprocessing should follow the dataset-specific rule in `config.yaml`
  - quantile transform by default
  - no preprocessing for Otto

#### 4.2 Objective and metric
- Classification: optimize / evaluate **accuracy**
- Regression: optimize / evaluate **RMSE**

The paper uses validation performance for tuning and test performance for final reporting.

#### 4.3 Hyperparameter space
Use the appendix-configured CatBoost space from `config.yaml`:
- `max_depth`: `[1, 10]`
- `learning_rate`: `[0.001, 1.0]`
- `bagging_temperature`: `[0.0, 1.0]`
- `l2_leaf_reg`: `[1.0, 10.0]`
- `leaf_estimation_iterations`: `[1, 10]`

Fixed parameters from config:
- `early_stopping_rounds = 50`
- `od_pval = 0.001`
- `iterations = 2000`

These fixed parameters must not be tuned.

#### 4.4 Categorical feature handling
CatBoost requires categorical indices or column references.
The module should ensure:
- correct categorical feature indices are passed
- no accidental transformation destroys categorical semantics
- evaluation uses the same split features as training

---

### 5) Data flow for XGBoost
XGBoost baseline follows the paper’s tuning protocol, but with special handling for MI and CO.

#### 5.1 Feature handling
XGBoost should use the same prepared feature matrix as the non-CatBoost baselines:
- numeric features already transformed according to dataset policy
- categorical features one-hot encoded if present in the preprocessing pipeline

XGBoost does not use built-in categorical support here because the paper’s protocol specifies one-hot encoding for non-CatBoost algorithms.

#### 5.2 Objective and metric
- Classification: use the correct XGBoost classification objective
- Regression: use regression objective
- Optimize validation metric corresponding to task:
  - accuracy
  - RMSE

Since the paper compares outcomes after validation-driven selection, the module should convert model predictions to class labels or continuous outputs as needed for metric computation.

#### 5.3 Dataset-specific device policy
From the paper and config:
- For tuning on **MI** and **CO**, use `GPU`
- In all other cases, use `CPU`
- The config explicitly notes this policy

This device logic must be applied only to tuning. The paper notes evaluation on those datasets also uses CPU in the visible excerpt, but the config text explicitly states:
- tuning on MI and CO: `GPU`
- all other cases, including evaluation on these two datasets: `CPU`

This means the runner must separate:
- tuning device choice
- final fit/eval device choice

If the implementation cannot support GPU reliably, it should still preserve the device-switching logic from config and fail clearly rather than silently changing behavior.

#### 5.4 Hyperparameter space
Use the XGBoost space from `config.yaml`:
- `max_depth`: `[3, 10]`
- `min_child_weight`: `[1e-4, 100.0]`
- `subsample`: `[0.5, 1.0]`
- `learning_rate`: `[0.001, 1.0]`
- `colsample_bytree`: `[0.5, 1.0]`
- `gamma`: `[0.0, 100.0]`
- `lambda`: `[0.0, 10.0]`

Fixed parameters from config:
- `booster = gbtree`
- `early_stopping_rounds = 50`
- `n_estimators = 2000`

These must remain fixed.

---

### 6) Tuning logic
Both CatBoost and XGBoost should be tuned using the same general principle:
1. Sample hyperparameters from the configured ranges.
2. Train on training split.
3. Evaluate on validation split.
4. Select the best configuration according to validation score.

Because the paper follows validation-based selection:
- never use test data for tuning
- use the validation score to determine the best trial

The module should cooperate with `training/tuning.py` or implement its own internal tuning logic if that is how the design routes baseline experiments. The key requirement is that the validation-driven selection matches the paper.

---

### 7) Final test evaluation
Once the best hyperparameters are selected:
- retrain the model using the selected configuration
- evaluate on the test split
- report the final metric

The paper’s baseline tables focus on final test performance. The baseline runner should therefore return:
- best validation score
- test score
- tuned hyperparameters
- optionally predictions if ensemble-level baseline processing is needed later

---

### 8) Metrics and output conventions
The module should use the centralized metric conventions from `evaluation/metrics.py`:
- `accuracy` for classification
- `rmse` for regression

For consistency, `GBDDBaselineRunner` should not invent its own metric semantics.

Expected output should be compatible with the rest of the experiment pipeline, e.g. a dictionary containing:
- dataset name
- model name (`CatBoost` or `XGBoost`)
- best validation metric
- test metric
- best hyperparameters
- maybe seed info if the broader pipeline repeats runs

---

### 9) Seed and reproducibility behavior
This module must rely on `utils/reproducibility.py` for deterministic control.

Important:
- use the same seed as the experiment config
- if multiple trials or repeated runs are executed, seed each run consistently
- do not introduce hidden randomness outside controlled seeds

CatBoost and XGBoost both have internal randomness; those should be seeded through their respective APIs whenever possible.

---

### 10) Paper-specific constraints to preserve
This module must respect the following paper constraints:

1. **CatBoost uses categorical features directly**
   - no one-hot encoding for CatBoost path

2. **XGBoost uses one-hot encoded categorical variables**
   - follows the non-CatBoost preprocessing policy

3. **MI and CO use GPU for XGBoost tuning**
   - as stated in the paper/config

4. **Early stopping**
   - `early_stopping_rounds = 50`

5. **Iteration budget**
   - `iterations = 2000` for CatBoost
   - `n_estimators = 2000` for XGBoost

6. **Target standardization**
   - regression targets should already be standardized according to config
   - the runner must not undo that

7. **Same split protocol**
   - train/val/test must remain consistent with the rest of the pipeline

---

### 11) Implementation dependencies
This file will depend on:
- `catboost`
- `xgboost`
- `numpy`
- `pandas`
- `sklearn.metrics`

It should also import:
- experiment config structure from the shared config utility
- reproducibility helpers
- the centralized metric evaluator if possible

Avoid adding unrelated dependencies.

---

### 12) Edge cases and failure handling
The runner should handle:
- datasets without categorical features
- regression vs classification objective switching
- CPU/GPU availability differences
- one-hot feature matrices with sparse/dense representations
- empty or degenerate validation improvement cases during early stopping

For Otto:
- ensure the preprocessing rule “none” is respected before CatBoost/XGBoost consumption

For MI:
- ranking is treated as regression, so XGBoost/CatBoost should use regression loss/metric

---

### 13) Recommended internal structure
Even though only `run_catboost()` and `run_xgboost()` are public by design, internally the module should logically separate:
- parameter sampling
- model construction
- fit/evaluate
- metric conversion
- device selection

This keeps the baseline reproduction faithful and testable.

---

### 14) Final reproducibility checklist for this file
Before coding, confirm the following logic is implemented:

- [ ] CatBoost uses native categorical support
- [ ] XGBoost uses one-hot categorical features
- [ ] Hyperparameter ranges match `config.yaml`
- [ ] Fixed iteration / early stopping values match `config.yaml`
- [ ] MI and CO XGBoost tuning uses GPU
- [ ] All validation selection is done without test leakage
- [ ] Output metrics use the shared evaluator
- [ ] Seed handling is centralized and deterministic

[ANALYSIS] main.py
============================================
### main.py Logic Analysis

`main.py` is the top-level orchestration entrypoint for reproducing the paper’s experiments. It must glue together configuration loading, reproducibility setup, data preparation, optional tuning, training, evaluation, ensembling, and optional GBDT baselines.

This file should **not** implement modeling logic itself; it should only coordinate the modules defined in the design and ensure every run follows the paper’s protocol and `config.yaml`.

---

## 1) Responsibilities of `main.py`

`main.py` should perform the following in order:

1. Load the experiment configuration from `config.yaml`
2. Set global seeds and deterministic behavior
3. Load the dataset and construct train/validation/test splits
4. Apply preprocessing according to dataset and model type
5. Optionally run Optuna hyperparameter tuning
6. Train the selected model using the best configuration
7. Repeat training/evaluation for the required number of seeds
8. Compute test metrics using the unified evaluator
9. Optionally build 3 ensembles from the 15 seed runs
10. Optionally run CatBoost/XGBoost baselines
11. Save all outputs: configs, tuned parameters, metrics, predictions, and summaries

---

## 2) Configuration-driven control flow

`main.py` must rely on `config.yaml` and never hardcode values that are specified there.

Important config values to use:

- `experiment.seed = 42`
- `experiment.num_seeds = 15`
- `experiment.num_ensembles = 3`
- `experiment.ensemble_group_size = 5`
- `training.optimizer = adamw`
- `training.loss.classification = cross_entropy`
- `training.loss.regression = mse`
- `training.early_stopping.patience = 16`
- `training.early_stopping.min_delta = 0.0`
- `training.lr_schedule = none`
- `training.max_epochs = 100`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.method = optuna_tpe`
- `hyperparameter_tuning.trials = 100`

It also must use dataset-specific settings from the config:

- preprocessing policy by dataset
- batch size by dataset
- transformer search spaces by dataset group
- baseline hyperparameter ranges

---

## 3) Inputs expected by `main.py`

`main.py` should assume the runtime receives at least:

- path to `config.yaml`
- dataset name
- model name
- task type
- optional flags for:
  - tuning on/off
  - ensemble evaluation on/off
  - baseline runs on/off

The exact CLI interface is not specified in the design, so `main.py` should remain simple and accept a minimal experiment-run specification consistent with the config schema.

---

## 4) Required orchestration steps

### Step 1: Load config
Use `utils.io.load_config(path)` to parse `config.yaml` into a structured experiment config object or dictionary.

The config must remain the source of truth for:
- dataset preprocessing
- training hyperparameters
- tuning search spaces
- evaluation settings
- baseline settings

---

### Step 2: Set reproducibility state
Before any stochastic operation:

- call `utils.reproducibility.set_seed(config.experiment.seed)`
- call `utils.reproducibility.set_deterministic()`
- resolve device through `utils.reproducibility.get_device(config.device)` or equivalent config field

This is required before:
- dataset splitting
- preprocessing fitting
- tuning
- model initialization
- training
- baseline fitting

---

### Step 3: Load and split dataset
Instantiate `DatasetLoader` from `data/dataset_loader.py`.

Call:
- `load_raw()`
- `split_data(df)` or `load_and_split()`

The loader must return:
- train/validation/test partitions
- numerical and categorical column lists
- dataset metadata needed downstream

`main.py` must not bypass the loader.

---

### Step 4: Preprocess data
Instantiate `Preprocessor` from `data/preprocessing.py`.

Call:
- `fit_transform(splits)` for training-time setup
- or `fit()` then `transform()` if reuse is needed

The preprocessing behavior must follow `config.yaml` and the paper:

- numerical:
  - default: quantile transform
  - Otto Group Product Classification: none
- categorical:
  - default: one-hot
- regression targets:
  - standardized

This preprocessing must be shared across all deep models for a given dataset.

---

### Step 5: Optional tuning
If tuning is enabled for the current experiment, `main.py` must:

1. Instantiate `training.tuning.Tuner`
2. Run Optuna TPE with `hyperparameter_tuning.trials = 100`
3. Tune only on validation performance
4. Select the best configuration by validation metric

The tuner must use the correct search spaces from `config.yaml`, including:

- MLP:
  - layers
  - layer size
  - dropout
  - weight decay
  - learning rate
- ResNet:
  - layers
  - layer size
  - hidden factor
  - hidden dropout
  - residual dropout
  - weight decay
  - learning rate
- Transformer:
  - dataset-group dependent layers, embedding size, residual dropout, weight decay
  - attention dropout
  - FFN dropout
  - FFN factor
  - learning rate
- Embedding-specific tuning:
  - linear embedding output dim
  - PLE quantiles
  - PLE tree parameters
  - periodic k

`main.py` should pass the dataset name and model name so the tuner can select the correct space.

---

### Step 6: Build model from factory
After tuning, or directly if tuning is disabled, `main.py` should call:

- `models.tabular_model.ModelFactory.create(...)`

This factory must interpret the model naming convention, such as:

- `MLP`
- `MLP-LR`
- `MLP-Q-LR`
- `Transformer-T-LR`
- `ResNet-PLR`

`main.py` should not map model strings manually if the factory already encapsulates this logic.

---

### Step 7: Train the model
Use `training.trainer.Trainer`.

The trainer must:
- use AdamW
- use the loss function appropriate for the task
- train for at most `training.max_epochs = 100`
- apply early stopping with `patience = 16`
- use `min_delta = 0.0`
- use no LR schedule
- checkpoint the best validation model

`main.py` should invoke training once per seed.

---

### Step 8: Repeat over 15 seeds
Per `config.yaml`, each tuned configuration must be evaluated over:

- `experiment.num_seeds = 15`

Therefore `main.py` should:
1. generate or iterate over 15 seeds
2. set the seed before each run
3. retrain the best configuration
4. collect test predictions and metrics
5. aggregate mean/std across runs

This is essential to match the paper’s reporting protocol.

---

### Step 9: Compute metrics
Use `evaluation.metrics.MetricsEvaluator`.

It must compute:
- classification: accuracy
- regression: RMSE

`main.py` should:
- use the validation metric during tuning
- use the test metric for final reporting
- keep the metric consistent with the task type

For Microsoft ranking data, the paper treats the task as regression; `main.py` should follow the config’s `task_type` rather than inventing special handling.

---

### Step 10: Ensemble evaluation
If ensemble evaluation is enabled:

- `experiment.num_ensembles = 3`
- `experiment.ensemble_group_size = 5`

`main.py` should gather the 15 seed-level predictions and pass them to `evaluation.ensemble.EnsembleEvaluator`.

The evaluator must:
- partition the 15 models into 3 disjoint groups of 5
- average predictions within each group
- compute ensemble metrics

`main.py` should then save both:
- per-seed metrics
- ensemble metrics

---

### Step 11: Optional GBDT baselines
If baseline evaluation is enabled, `main.py` should invoke:

- `baselines.gbdt.GBDDBaselineRunner.run_catboost(...)`
- `baselines.gbdt.GBDDBaselineRunner.run_xgboost(...)`

The baselines must respect config settings:

#### CatBoost
Use fixed values:
- `early_stopping_rounds = 50`
- `od_pval = 0.001`
- `iterations = 2000`

Tune:
- `max_depth`
- `learning_rate`
- `bagging_temperature`
- `l2_leaf_reg`
- `leaf_estimation_iterations`

#### XGBoost
Use fixed values:
- `booster = gbtree`
- `early_stopping_rounds = 50`
- `n_estimators = 2000`

Tune:
- `max_depth`
- `min_child_weight`
- `subsample`
- `learning_rate`
- `colsample_bytree`
- `gamma`
- `lambda`

`main.py` should not duplicate baseline tuning logic.

---

## 5) Output artifacts to save

`main.py` should save reproducible artifacts using `utils.io`:

- resolved config
- best hyperparameters
- per-seed metrics
- ensemble metrics
- baseline metrics
- predictions if needed for ensembling or later inspection
- run metadata such as dataset, model, seed list, device

This is important for paper-style reproducibility and debugging.

Suggested outputs:
- JSON summary files for metrics
- pickle files for tuned configs or prediction arrays
- checkpoints for best model weights
- logs for tuning and training progress

---

## 6) Control flow structure

A clean orchestration pattern for `main.py` is:

1. parse config and runtime args
2. set reproducibility
3. load raw data
4. split data
5. preprocess data
6. optionally tune
7. for each seed in 15 seeds:
   - reset seed
   - build model
   - train model
   - evaluate on test
   - store predictions and metric
8. aggregate mean/std
9. optionally ensemble
10. optionally run baselines
11. save all results

---

## 7) Important invariants from the paper

`main.py` must enforce these paper constraints:

- test set is never used in tuning
- preprocessing is consistent across deep models for a dataset
- Otto has no feature preprocessing
- regression targets are standardized
- no learning-rate schedules
- early stopping patience is 16
- 15 runs per tuned configuration
- feature embeddings are handled per feature, not by mixing raw numerical inputs
- evaluation metric follows task type
- CatBoost and XGBoost comparisons should use the same data splits and target preprocessing

---

## 8) What `main.py` should not do

`main.py` should avoid implementing:

- binning logic for PLE
- periodic embedding math
- backbone layer definitions
- metric formulas
- optimizer internals
- preprocessing transformer details

Those belong in their dedicated modules.

---

## 9) Minimal dependency map

`main.py` depends on:

- `utils.io`
- `utils.reproducibility`
- `data.dataset_loader`
- `data.preprocessing`
- `models.tabular_model`
- `training.tuner`
- `training.trainer`
- `evaluation.metrics`
- `evaluation.ensemble`
- `baselines.gbdt`

It should coordinate them in that order.

---

## 10) Reproduction fidelity notes

A faithful `main.py` should preserve the paper’s experiment hierarchy:

- first tune on validation
- then retrain/evaluate across 15 seeds
- then optionally ensemble
- then optionally run GBDT baselines

If there is any ambiguity in dataset splits or Microsoft fold handling, `main.py` should defer to the dataset loader rather than embedding custom logic.

---

## 11) Final implementation intent

`main.py` is the experiment conductor. Its correctness is measured by whether it can reproduce the paper’s full protocol end-to-end with minimal user interaction and without violating any config or design constraint.

The entrypoint should make the entire system usable as a single command for:

- baseline DL experiments
- embedding ablations
- ensemble comparisons
- GBDT comparisons
- reproducible reporting across all 11 datasets

## utils/reproducibility.py
"""Utilities for deterministic and reproducible experiment execution.

This module centralizes seed control and device resolution for the project.
It intentionally keeps the public API minimal:

- set_seed(seed)
- set_deterministic()
- get_device(device)

The implementation is designed to support reproducible tabular deep learning
experiments with repeated random seeds, Optuna tuning, and optional CUDA
execution.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import os
import random
from typing import Optional, Union

import numpy as np
import torch


def set_seed(seed: int) -> None:
    """Seeds Python, NumPy, and PyTorch RNGs.

    Args:
      seed: Integer seed value to apply across all supported RNGs.

    Raises:
      TypeError: If `seed` is not an integer-like value.
      ValueError: If `seed` is outside the valid range for Python/NumPy/PyTorch
        seeding on the current platform.
    """
    if not isinstance(seed, int):
        raise TypeError(f"Expected `seed` to be an int, got {type(seed).__name__}.")

    # Keep Python hash-based structures reproducible as much as possible.
    os.environ["PYTHONHASHSEED"] = str(seed)

    random.seed(seed)
    np.random.seed(seed)

    torch.manual_seed(seed)
    if torch.cuda.is_available():
        torch.cuda.manual_seed(seed)
        torch.cuda.manual_seed_all(seed)


def set_deterministic() -> None:
    """Configures PyTorch for deterministic execution as much as possible.

    This function should be called once at startup before model creation or
    training. It does not alter any experiment logic; it only adjusts backend
    behavior to reduce nondeterminism.

    Notes:
      - Some operations may still be unsupported in deterministic mode.
      - The function prefers reproducibility over permissive execution.
    """
    # Encourage deterministic CUDA behavior.
    os.environ.setdefault("CUBLAS_WORKSPACE_CONFIG", ":4096:8")

    # cuDNN settings.
    torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False
    torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True

    # Use deterministic algorithms where supported.
    # The warning-only mode is preferred to avoid hard crashes on environments
    # where certain ops may not have deterministic implementations.
    try:
        torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True, warn_only=True)
    except TypeError:
        # Older PyTorch versions may not support warn_only.
        torch.use_deterministic_algorithms(True)

    # Disable TF32 to reduce numerical variability on newer GPUs.
    if hasattr(torch.backends, "cuda") and hasattr(torch.backends.cuda, "matmul"):
        torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_tf32 = False
    if hasattr(torch.backends, "cudnn"):
        torch.backends.cudnn.allow_tf32 = False


def get_device(device: Union[str, torch.device, None]) -> torch.device:
    """Resolves a requested device string to a concrete PyTorch device.

    Args:
      device: A device specification such as "cpu", "cuda", "cuda:0",
        torch.device, or None.

    Returns:
      A torch.device object. If CUDA is requested but unavailable, CPU is
      returned safely.

    Examples:
      >>> get_device("cpu")
      device(type='cpu')
      >>> get_device("cuda")
      device(type='cuda')  # only if CUDA is available
    """
    if device is None:
        return torch.device("cpu")

    if isinstance(device, torch.device):
        requested_device = device
    elif isinstance(device, str):
        requested_device = torch.device(device)
    else:
        raise TypeError(
            f"Expected `device` to be a str, torch.device, or None, got "
            f"{type(device).__name__}."
        )

    if requested_device.type == "cuda":
        if torch.cuda.is_available():
            # Preserve the requested CUDA index if provided; otherwise use cuda:0.
            index: Optional[int] = requested_device.index
            return torch.device("cuda", index if index is not None else 0)
        return torch.device("cpu")

    return torch.device("cpu")
```

"""Utilities for loading configuration and persisting experiment artifacts.

This module provides a minimal, robust I/O layer for the reproduction codebase.
It is intentionally self-contained to avoid circular imports and to keep config
handling consistent across training, tuning, and evaluation modules.

Public API:
  - load_config(path)
  - save_json(obj, path)
  - save_pickle(obj, path)
  - load_pickle(path)

The loader validates the presence of required configuration sections from the
provided `config.yaml` and returns a nested dictionary structure with the exact
values from the file.
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import json
import pickle
from dataclasses import dataclass
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Any, Dict, Iterable, Mapping, MutableMapping, Union

import yaml


PathLike = Union[str, Path]
ConfigDict = Dict[str, Any]


_REQUIRED_TOP_LEVEL_KEYS = (
    "experiment",
    "data",
    "training",
    "hyperparameter_tuning",
    "models",
    "evaluation",
    "baselines",
)

_REQUIRED_KEY_PATHS = (
    ("experiment", "seed"),
    ("experiment", "num_seeds"),
    ("experiment", "num_ensembles"),
    ("experiment", "ensemble_group_size"),
    ("data", "preprocessing", "numerical", "default"),
    (
        "data",
        "preprocessing",
        "numerical",
        "otto_group_product_classification",
    ),
    ("data", "preprocessing", "categorical", "default"),
    ("data", "target", "regression_standardization"),
    ("training", "optimizer"),
    ("training", "loss", "classification"),
    ("training", "loss", "regression"),
    ("training", "learning_rate", "mlp"),
    ("training", "learning_rate", "resnet"),
    ("training", "learning_rate", "transformer", "sa_co_mi"),
    ("training", "learning_rate", "transformer", "other"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ge"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ch"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ca"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ho"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ad"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "ot"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "hi"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "fb"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "sa"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "co"),
    ("training", "batch_size", "mi"),
    ("training", "early_stopping", "patience"),
    ("training", "early_stopping", "min_delta"),
    ("training", "lr_schedule"),
    ("training", "max_epochs"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "method"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "trials"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "search_spaces", "linear_embedding_output_dim"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "search_spaces", "ple_quantiles"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "search_spaces", "ple_tree", "max_leaves"),
    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "search_spaces", "ple_tree", "min_items_per_leaf"),
    (
        "hyperparameter_tuning",

        "ple_tree",
        "min_information_gain",

    ("hyperparameter_tuning", "search_spaces", "periodic_k"),
    ("models", "mlp", "layers"),

    ("models", "resnet", "hidden_factor"),
    ("models", "resnet", "hidden_dropout"),
    ("models", "resnet", "residual_dropout"),
    ("models", "resnet", "weight_decay"),
    ("models", "transformer", "layers", "sa_co_mi"),
    ("models", "transformer", "layers", "other"),
    ("models", "transformer", "embedding_size", "sa_co_mi"),
    ("models", "transformer", "embedding_size", "other"),
    ("models", "transformer", "residual_dropout", "sa_co_mi"),
    ("models", "transformer", "residual_dropout", "other"),
    ("models", "transformer", "attention_dropout"),

    ("models", "transformer", "weight_decay", "other"),
    ("evaluation", "metrics", "classification"),
    ("evaluation", "metrics", "regression"),
    ("evaluation", "validation_selection"),
    ("evaluation", "test_runs_per_tuned_config"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "fixed", "early_stopping_rounds"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "fixed", "od_pval"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "fixed", "iterations"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "tuning", "max_depth"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "tuning", "learning_rate"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "tuning", "bagging_temperature"),
    ("baselines", "catboost", "tuning", "l2_leaf_reg"),

    ("baselines", "xgboost", "fixed", "booster"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "fixed", "early_stopping_rounds"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "fixed", "n_estimators"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "max_depth"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "min_child_weight"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "subsample"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "learning_rate"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "colsample_bytree"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "gamma"),
    ("baselines", "xgboost", "tuning", "lambda"),
)


def _to_path(path: PathLike) -> Path:
    """Converts a string/path-like input to a `Path` object."""
    if isinstance(path, Path):
        return path
    if isinstance(path, str):
        return Path(path)
    raise TypeError(f"Expected path as str or Path, got {type(path).__name__}.")


def _get_nested_value(data: Mapping[str, Any], key_path: Iterable[str]) -> Any:
    """Returns a nested value from a mapping or raises KeyError with context."""
    current: Any = data
    traversed_keys = []
    for key in key_path:
        traversed_keys.append(key)
        if not isinstance(current, Mapping):
            joined = ".".join(traversed_keys[:-1])
            raise KeyError(
                f"Expected mapping at '{joined}', but found {type(current).__name__}."
            )
        if key not in current:
            raise KeyError(f"Missing required config key: '{'.'.join(traversed_keys)}'")
        current = current[key]
    return current


def _validate_config(config: Mapping[str, Any]) -> None:
    """Validates the loaded config against the expected experiment schema."""
    if not isinstance(config, Mapping):
        raise TypeError(
            f"Configuration must be a mapping, got {type(config).__name__}."
        )

    for top_key in _REQUIRED_TOP_LEVEL_KEYS:
        if top_key not in config:
            raise KeyError(f"Missing required top-level config section: '{top_key}'")

    for key_path in _REQUIRED_KEY_PATHS:
        _get_nested_value(config, key_path)

    # Consistency checks grounded in the provided config.yaml.
    num_seeds = int(_get_nested_value(config, ("experiment", "num_seeds")))
    num_ensembles = int(_get_nested_value(config, ("experiment", "num_ensembles")))
    ensemble_group_size = int(
        _get_nested_value(config, ("experiment", "ensemble_group_size"))
    )
    if num_seeds != 15:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected experiment.num_seeds to be 15 for the paper protocol, got {num_seeds}."
        )
    if num_ensembles != 3:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected experiment.num_ensembles to be 3, got {num_ensembles}."
        )
    if ensemble_group_size != 5:
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected experiment.ensemble_group_size to be 5, got {ensemble_group_size}."
        )
    if num_seeds != num_ensembles * ensemble_group_size:
        raise ValueError(
            "Inconsistent ensemble settings: num_seeds must equal "
            "num_ensembles * ensemble_group_size."
        )

    lr_schedule = _get_nested_value(config, ("training", "lr_schedule"))
    if lr_schedule != "none":
        raise ValueError(
            f"Expected training.lr_schedule to be 'none', got {lr_schedule!r}."
        )

    otto_preproc = _get_nested_value(
        config,
        ("data", "preprocessing", "numerical", "otto_group_product_classification"),
    )
    if otto_preproc != "none":
        raise ValueError(
            "Expected Otto Group Product Classification numerical preprocessing to be "
            f"'none', got {otto_preproc!r}."
        )

    test_runs = int(
        _get_nested_value(config, ("evaluation", "test_runs_per_tuned_config"))
    )
    if test_runs != num_seeds:
        raise ValueError(
            "evaluation.test_runs_per_tuned_config must match experiment.num_seeds "
            f"({num_seeds}), got {test_runs}."
        )


def _read_text_file(path: Path) -> str:
    """Reads a UTF-8 text file and returns its contents."""
    if not path.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"Config file does not exist: {path}")
    if not path.is_file():
        raise IsADirectoryError(f"Expected a file path, got directory: {path}")
    return path.read_text(encoding="utf-8")


def load_config(path: PathLike) -> ConfigDict:
    """Loads a YAML or JSON configuration file and validates its schema.

    Args:
      path: Path to a `.yaml`, `.yml`, or `.json` configuration file.

    Returns:
      A nested dictionary containing the experiment configuration.

    Raises:
      FileNotFoundError: If the config file does not exist.
      ValueError: If the file cannot be parsed or fails validation.
      TypeError: If the parsed content is not a mapping.
    """
    config_path = _to_path(path)
    raw_text = _read_text_file(config_path)

    parsed: Any = None
    suffix = config_path.suffix.lower()

    try:
        if suffix in {".yaml", ".yml"}:
            parsed = yaml.safe_load(raw_text)
        elif suffix == ".json":
            parsed = json.loads(raw_text)
        else:
            # Try YAML first because it is a superset of JSON in practice.
            try:
                parsed = yaml.safe_load(raw_text)
            except yaml.YAMLError:
                parsed = json.loads(raw_text)
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover - explicit rethrow with context
        raise ValueError(f"Failed to parse configuration file '{config_path}': {exc}") from exc

    if parsed is None:
        raise ValueError(f"Configuration file '{config_path}' is empty.")
    if not isinstance(parsed, Mapping):
        raise TypeError(
            f"Configuration root must be a mapping, got {type(parsed).__name__}."
        )

    config: ConfigDict = dict(parsed)
    _validate_config(config)
    return config


def _json_default(obj: Any) -> Any:
    """Best-effort JSON serializer for common scientific Python objects."""
    if isinstance(obj, Path):
        return str(obj)

    if hasattr(obj, "item") and callable(getattr(obj, "item")):
        try:
            return obj.item()
        except Exception:
            pass

    if hasattr(obj, "tolist") and callable(getattr(obj, "tolist")):
        try:
            return obj.tolist()
        except Exception:
            pass

    if hasattr(obj, "__dict__"):
        return obj.__dict__

    raise TypeError(f"Object of type {type(obj).__name__} is not JSON serializable.")


def save_json(obj: Any, path: PathLike) -> None:
    """Saves an object as JSON, creating parent directories if needed.

    Args:
      obj: JSON-serializable object or a structure convertible by `_json_default`.
      path: Destination file path.

    Raises:
      ValueError: If JSON serialization fails.
    """
    json_path = _to_path(path)
    json_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    try:
        with json_path.open("w", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            json.dump(
                obj,
                f,
                indent=2,
                sort_keys=True,
                ensure_ascii=False,
                default=_json_default,
            )
            f.write("\n")
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover - explicit rethrow with context
        raise ValueError(f"Failed to save JSON to '{json_path}': {exc}") from exc


def save_pickle(obj: Any, path: PathLike) -> None:
    """Saves an object to a pickle file, creating parent directories if needed."""
    pickle_path = _to_path(path)
    pickle_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    try:
        with pickle_path.open("wb") as f:
            pickle.dump(obj, f, protocol=pickle.HIGHEST_PROTOCOL)
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover - explicit rethrow with context
        raise ValueError(f"Failed to save pickle to '{pickle_path}': {exc}") from exc


def load_pickle(path: PathLike) -> Any:
    """Loads a pickled object from disk.

    Args:
      path: Path to a `.pkl`, `.pickle`, or other pickle-formatted file.

    Returns:
      The deserialized Python object.

    Raises:
      FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
      ValueError: If unpickling fails.
    """
    pickle_path = _to_path(path)
    if not pickle_path.exists():
        raise FileNotFoundError(f"Pickle file does not exist: {pickle_path}")
    if not pickle_path.is_file():
        raise IsADirectoryError(f"Expected a file path, got directory: {pickle_path}")

    try:
        with pickle_path.open("rb") as f:
            return pickle.load(f)
    except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover - explicit rethrow with context
        raise ValueError(f"Failed to load pickle from '{pickle_path}': {exc}") from exc
```
